AdapTT: Functoriality for Dependent Type Casts
Arthur Adjedj, Meven Lennon-Bertrand, Thibaut Benjamin, Kenji Maillard

Abstract
. The ability to cast values between related types is a leitmotiv of many flavors of dependent type theory, such as observational type theories, subtyping, or cast calculi for gradual typing. These casts all exhibit a common structural behavior that boils down to the pervasive functoriality of type formers. We propose and extensively study a type theory, called AdapTT, which makes systematic and precise this idea of functorial type formers, with respect to an abstract notion of adapters relating types. Leveraging descriptions for functorial inductive types in AdapTT, we derive structural laws for type casts on general inductive type formers.

1.
Introduction

Type casting
is a fundamental operation in typed programming languages,
turning a value of one type into a value of another to mediate between
superficially different types.
In the setting of proof assistants and
dependent type systems, where types are extremely rich and precise, the need for
such mediation is particularly dire.
Proof assistants thus offer many type casting mechanisms: coercions
Saïbi, 1997
are heavily employed in
Rocq

Team, 2025
and
Lean

Moura and Ullrich, 2021
, in particular to deal with hierarchies of structures
Garillot et al., 2009
;
Cohen et al., 2020
; predicate subtyping
Shankar and Owre, 2000
is a central tool for verifying properties in PVS
Owre et al., 1992
, the Russel sub-language of
Rocq

Sozeau, 2007
, and F
\star

Swamy et al., 2016b
; and cumulativity
Luo, 1989
, present by default in
Rocq
and optionally in
Agda

4
, greatly simplifies the management of universe levels.

A particularly attractive feature of type casting is to be
structural
,
meaning that type casts can be systematically lifted through type formers.
Structural type casts can be applied deeply in types, rather than be confined
to the top level, which makes them much more powerful and modular.
And indeed, failures of typecast to properly lift through type formers
manifest as painful errors that can be hard to debug
Laurent, 2025
, p.35

or expressivity limitations
Hrițcu, 2014
.
Structural type casting is an important feature in recent work such as
Observational Type Theory (TT
obs
)
Altenkirch et al., 2007
;
Pujet and Tabareau, 2022
, dependent type theories with coercive subtyping (MLTT
coe
)
Luo and Adams, 2008
;
Laurent et al., 2024
, or cast calculi for gradual dependent types (CastCIC)
Lennon-Bertrand et al., 2022
.

Footnote

1
shows the structural type casts between
function types (
\Pi
) in these three systems.
Although the exact mechanisms differ, in all cases the cast of a
function
f
proceeds by first casting its argument before passing it to
f
,
and finally casting the result back.
Such striking similarities can be observed with structural casts for other type formers too.
In this paper, we argue that this common core is no coincidence, but emerges
from a deeper structure:

Structural type casts arise from functorial type formers.

\frac{\Gamma\vdash e\mathrel{:}\P x:A\mathchar 58\relax B=\P x:A^{\prime}\mathchar 58\relax B^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash f\mathrel{:}\P x:A\mathchar 58\relax B\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash a^{\prime}\mathrel{:}A^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pta\coloneq\operatorname{cast}{}(A^{\prime},A,\operatorname{fst}(e),a^{\prime})}{\Gamma\vdash\operatorname{cast}{}(\P x:A\mathchar 58\relax B,\P x:A^{\prime}\mathchar 58\relax B^{\prime},e,f)~a^{\prime}\equiv\operatorname{cast}{}(\mathord{B}[a],\mathord{B^{\prime}}[a^{\prime}],\operatorname{snd}(e),f\,a)\mathrel{:}\mathord{B}[a]}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}\frac{\Gamma\vdash A^{\prime}\sub A\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma,x:A^{\prime}\vdash\mathord{B}[\coeop_{A^{\prime},A}x]\sub B^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash f\mathrel{:}\P x:A\mathchar 58\relax B\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash a^{\prime}\mathrel{:}A^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pta\coloneq\coeop_{A^{\prime},A}a^{\prime}}{\Gamma\vdash(\coeop_{\P x:A\mathchar 58\relax B,\P x:A^{\prime}\mathchar 58\relax B^{\prime}}f)~a^{\prime}\equiv\coeop_{\mathord{B}[a],\mathord{B^{\prime}}[a^{\prime}]}(f\,a)\mathrel{:}\mathord{B^{\prime}}[a^{\prime}]}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}\frac{\Gamma\vdash A\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash A^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma,x:A\vdash B\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma,x:A^{\prime}\vdash B^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash f\mathrel{:}\P x:A\mathchar 58\relax B\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash a^{\prime}\mathrel{:}A^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pta\coloneq\langle A\Leftarrow A^{\prime}\rangle\,a^{\prime}}{\Gamma\vdash\left(\langle\P x:A^{\prime}\mathchar 58\relax B^{\prime}\Leftarrow\P x:A\mathchar 58\relax B\rangle\,f\right)~a^{\prime}\equiv\langle\mathord{B^{\prime}}[a^{\prime}]\Leftarrow\mathord{B}[a]\rangle\left(f~a\right)\mathrel{:}\mathord{B^{\prime}}[a^{\prime}]}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}

Casts between
\P
types in observational equality,
subtyping and cast calculi for gradual typing.

1

To highlight their similarity, we express these rules as definitional equalities,
which are inter-derivable with the original rules from

Pujet (2022)
;
Laurent et al. (2024)
;
Lennon-Bertrand et al. (2022)

in presence of β and η for functions.

We see that the three rules are very similar.

Indeed, the three examples of
Footnote

1
all rely on a functor structure on the
\P
type former, a structure independently described in any model of

\symsf{MLTT}
by
Castellan et al. (2017, Lemma 4.8)
.
In each case, this functorial structure applies to different notions of morphisms between types: observational equalities in TT
obs
, subtyping coercions in MLTT
coe
and arbitrary cast in CastCIC.
This approach extends far beyond
\P
:
Laurent et al. (2024)
observe that many usual type formers (
\P,\Sig,\Id,\W,+,\List,\dots
) can be equipped with a functorial
action on functions that definitionally satisfies functoriality equations,
while keeping decidability of definitional equality and typing.

Challenges of structural type casting.

Multiple difficulties arise if one tries to unify diverse instances of structural type casts.
First, type casting can apply to varied notions of morphism between types,
as shown in the previous examples, and a unifying framework ought to allow for this diversity.
Second, type formers can introduce contravariant type casts, as exemplified by the domain of function types, and mixing variance and type dependency quickly becomes a subtle matter.
Third,
Laurent et al. (2024)
observed a strong obstruction to derive the functoriality of general inductive types
encoded via the composition of atomic type formers (
\Pi,\Sigma,\W,\ldots
).
The last two difficulties hamper the design of a general schema for inductive type formers
—as can be found in day-to-day proof assistants (
Rocq
,
Lean
,
Agda
)— that moreover supports structural type cast.

Introducing AdapTT

To attack these challenges, we take a step back and provide a solid and general framework to understand cast operations,
functoriality of type formers, and the relationship between the two. The
dependent type theory we develop, AdapTT, comes
equipped with a primitive notion of type morphisms,

adapters

McBride and Nordvall Forsberg, 2021
, which capture the data necessary to construct a cast.
The action of adapters on terms —type casting— is functorial with respect to the categorical structure adapters give to types. As a design principle, any type former in AdapTT should respect this structure as well,
i.e.
be functorial.

We show that AdapTT is able to encompass the above
examples of type theories with structural type casts.
We provide a corresponding notion of model, a variant of natural models
Awodey, 2018
dubbed NatMod
DO
s, and exhibit multiple instances of this structure.
Moreover, we establish a correspondence with the works of
Coraglia and Emmenegger (2024b)
;
Najmaei et al. (2026)
that develop related semantic notions based on comprehension categories
Jacobs, 1993
.

Functorial type formers with AdapTT
2

While AdapTT provides an adequate framework to state that a given type former is functorial
on a case-by-case basis, it does not provide the means to generically represent
the categories with respect to which a type former is functorial,
nor to derive that complex, composite types are structurally functorial.
We thus extend AdapTT with type variables which provides an internalization of type formers, including their action on adapters.
Contexts in this extension, AdapTT
2
, naturally exhibit a 2-categorical structure, which harmoniously interacts with our category of types and adapters.

AdapTT
2
gives us the tools we need to construct a theory of signatures in the style of Kaposi, Kovács et al.
Kovács, 2022
;
Kaposi and von Raumer, 2020
;
Kaposi et al., 2019
to describe general inductive type formers.
An inductive type described with such a signature is by construction functorial with respect to its parameters, described as a context in AdapTT
2
, entailing
a well-behaved notion of structural casts.
We illustrate the expressivity of our signatures by describing many standard examples,
and their functorial structure:
lists, sum types, W types, or the inductive identity type.

Contributions

We bring the following to the study of type casts in dependent type theory:

•

In
Section

2
, we introduce AdapTT, a type theory with type casts,
and NatMod
DO
, a categorical structure in which it interprets, which we relate to
previous work
Coraglia and Emmenegger, 2024b
;
Najmaei et al., 2026
;

•

Section

3
extends AdapTT to a 2-dimensional type theory, AdapTT
2
, giving the ability to internalize type formers using type variables, and
exhibiting important constraints on the allowed interactions of type variance and dependency;

•

We moreover show in
Section

3.3
that the
2
-category of (
\Cat
-valued) presheaves on such a NatMod
DO

\mathcal{C}
naturally interprets AdapTT
2
, giving rise to functorial type former on
\mathcal{C}
.

•

Finally,
Section

4
describes a variance-aware theory of signatures,
deriving structural type casts for inductive types through their inherent functorial action on adapters.

We managed to check most rules in isolation, but dire performance issues
prevented us from checking them all together.

rules of the paper in
Agda

Adjedj et al., 2025
.
The point of this formalisation was not to verify properties
of the system, rather,
Agda
was used as a guide to ensure the
numerous rules were type-correct.
This only provides partial support for our work,
but we still believe
Agda
’s roles was valuable enough to be acknowledged.

Adjedj et al., 2026
published
at POPL ’26.

2.
A Category of Types

This section introduces AdapTT, a dependent type theory with extra structure
for type casts.

Section

2.1
presents its syntax
while
Section

2.2
defines its categorical models, with examples.

Section

2.3
relates AdapTT to
an existing notion of model for (relevant) subtyping: gCwF
Coraglia and Emmenegger, 2024b
.

2

\Gamma\vdash\Box\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow B

for

between two types.
This terminology, borrowed from
McBride and Nordvall Forsberg (2021)
, emphasizes that
adapters provide information to transform values from one type to another.

omitting some standard rules given in

2Judgments and rules for AdapTT (excerpt, see )

In the context of structural subtyping,

(explicit) coercive subtyping requires exactly these functorial laws.

Example 2.1 (Function type in AdapTT).

AdapTT provides the judgments to state functoriality of type formers when adding
them to the system.
Illustrating this process on
\P
-types, for which we assume the usual
\symsf{MLTT}
rules,
one can express the data required to build an adapter and how this adapter acts on terms of
\P
type,
which constitute the common core of
Footnote

1
.

\frac{\Gamma\vdash a\mathrel{:}A^{\prime}\Rightarrow A\quad\quad\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}A^{\prime}\vdash b\mathrel{:}\mathord{B}[\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{tm}}\langle a\rangle]\Rightarrow B^{\prime}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathord{\P}\llbracket a\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}b\rrbracket\mathrel{:}\P A\mathchar 58\relax B\Rightarrow\P A^{\prime}\mathchar 58\relax B^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}\frac{\Gamma\vdash f\mathrel{:}\P A\mathchar 58\relax B\quad\Gamma\vdash u\mathrel{:}A^{\prime}\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash a\mathrel{:}A^{\prime}\Rightarrow A\quad\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}A^{\prime}\vdash b\mathrel{:}\mathord{B}[\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{tm}}\langle a\rangle]\Rightarrow B^{\prime}}{\Gamma\vdash(f\langle\mathord{\P}\llbracket a\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}b\rrbracket\rangle)\;u\equiv(f\,(u\langle a\rangle))\langle\mathord{b}[u]\rangle\mathrel{:}\mathord{B^{\prime}}[u]}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}

Each of the examples of
Footnote

1
can be represented by
making a different choice for what constitutes an adapter, which is made
possible by having a separate, dedicated sort of adapters.

For datatypes, structural adapters behave like generalized map functions, so for instance

(h\mathbin{:\!:}t)\langle\List(f)\rangle\equiv(h\langle f\rangle)\mathbin{:\!:}(t\langle\List(f)\rangle)

Our goal in
Section

4.2
will be to derive these
functorial adapters for large classes of datatypes, together with the appropriate
computation rules such as the above.

2.2.
Models for AdapTT

AdapTT is naturally interpreted in a variation on natural models
Awodey, 2018
,
which we introduce.
For a category
C
, we write
\Psh(C)=C^{\op}{\to}\cSet
for the presheaves over
C
,
and
\Pst(C)=C^{\op}{\to}\Cat
the
2
-category of
\Cat
-valued presheaves
(or pre-stacks) over
C
.

Definition 2.2 (NatMod
DO
).

A
natural model with discrete opfibration
(NatMod
DO
)
consists of a category
\Ctx
with a terminal object
\mathord{\diamondsuit}
,
two
\Cat
-valued presheaves
\Ty,\Tm\mathrel{:}\Ctx^{\op}\to\Cat

and a representable natural transformation
p\mathrel{:}\Tm\overset{\mathchar 58\relax}{\to}{}\Ty

equipped with a discrete opfibration structure in the
2
-category

\Pst(\Ctx)
.

By forgetting some of its structure, any NatMod
DO
is also a natural model in
the standard sense.
The representability of
p
, a key insight of
Awodey (2018)

(and, independently,
Fiore (2012)
),
characterizes context extension
\mathbin{\triangleright}
, weakening
\mathord{\uparrow}
and the variable
\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{tm}}
,
as a pullback in
\Pst(\Ctx)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\yon(\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Tm}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\yon\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ty}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{tm}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y\mathord{\uparrow}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}

where
\yon\mathrel{:}\Ctx\to\Pst(\Ctx)
is the Yoneda embedding.
4
4

4

The fact that
p
is a discrete opfibration in the
2
-category
\Pst(C)

ensures that the strict pullback is a
2
-pullback
Street, 1974
,
so that all
2
-categorical constructions are well-behaved.

Unfolding the definition of a discrete opfibration shows that
p
is
a discrete opfibration objectwise, together with a naturality condition. The
objectiwise structure gives, for any context
\Gamma\mathrel{:}\Ctx
and term
t\mathrel{:}\Tm(\Gamma)
, an adapter
a\mathrel{:}p_{\Gamma}\,t\to A^{\prime}
in
\Ty(\Gamma)
uniquely lifts through
p_{\Gamma}
, inducing some
\bar{a}\mathrel{:}t\to t^{\prime}
in
\Tm(\Gamma)
that maps to
a
through
p_{\Gamma}
.
The existence of the lifting interprets Rule
2Judgments and rules for AdapTT (excerpt, see )
, with
t^{\prime}
above
being
t\langle a\rangle
,
while the uniqueness validates Rule
2Judgments and rules for AdapTT (excerpt, see )

and
2Judgments and rules for AdapTT (excerpt, see )
. The
naturality condition of
p
in the discrete opfibration structure in the

2
-category
\Pst(\Ctx)
corresponds exactly to the validation of Rule
2Judgments and rules for AdapTT (excerpt, see )
.
Collecting these observations, we obtain the following theorem.

Theorem 2.3 (Interpretation of AdapTT).

AdapTT interprets soundly into any NatMod
DO
.

Proof.

AdapTT can be presented as a Second-Order Generalized Algebraic Theory (SOGAT) (see
Fig.

12
) that makes very explicit that it consists of a sort of types equipped with a category structure, and a functorial action on the dependent sort of terms.
The presentation of
Fig.

2
has been obtained with an explicit SOGAT-to-GAT translation
Kaposi and Xie, 2024
.
Following
Uemura (2021)
, a model of that SOGAT consists of a small category
\Ctx
with a terminal object, together with a presheaf of types
\Ty\mathrel{:}\Psh(\Ctx)
equipped with an internal category structure, a presheaf
\Tm\mathrel{:}\Psh(\Ctx)
, and a natural transformation
\Tm\overset{\mathchar 58\relax}{\to}\Ty
equipped with the structure of an internal discrete opfibration.
To finish the proof, observe that internal categories in
\Psh(\Ctx)
are equivalent to
\Cat
-valued presheaves
\Pst(\Ctx)
, so this data exactly amounts to that of
Definition

2.2
.
∎

The proof of
Theorem

2.3
also shows a form of completeness of AdapTT since the notion of models of AdapTT in its SOGAT presentation coincides with NatMod
DO
s.
A notion of completeness including initiality of the syntax
would require a discussion of morphisms of models that could preserve all the
structure on the nose or only up to an isomorphism.

NatMod
DO
s have many interesting examples. The admissible rules that we identify for some of these models (
Examples

2.8
and
2.9
) could more directly be used to
define
type theories with structural type casts, using AdapTT as a framework informing their design.

Example 2.4 (Natural models are discrete NatMod
DO
s).

Since any set is trivially a category with only identity morphisms,
any natural model is trivially a NatMod
DO
with only identity adapters.
Indeed, if
\Ty(\Gamma)
is a discrete category,
p
is automatically
a discrete opfibration.

Example 2.5 (
\cSet
as a NatMod
DO
).

The standard natural model on
\cSet
extends to a non-discrete NatMod
DO
.
Setting
\Ctx\coloneq\cSet
, contexts are interpreted as sets and substitutions as functions.
Types over a set
\Gamma
are functors
\Ty(\Gamma)\coloneq\operatorname{discr}\Gamma{\to}\cSet
from the discrete category
\operatorname{discr}\Gamma
to
\cSet
.
Terms over
\Gamma
are functors
\Tm(\Gamma)\coloneq\operatorname{discr}\Gamma{\to}\cSet_{\bullet}
to the category of pointed set, and
p
is the post-composition by the functor
\cSet_{\bullet}{\to}\cSet
forgetting the point.
Context extension is inherited from the natural model:
the extension of a set
\Gamma
with a family
A\mathrel{:}\Gamma{\to}\cSet
is given by the set
\Sigma(\gamma\mathrel{:}\Gamma)\mathchar 58\relax(A\,\gamma)
.
Expliciting the morphisms of
\operatorname{discr}\Gamma{\to}\cSet
, an adapter
\Gamma\vdash f\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow B
between
A,B\mathrel{:}\Ty(\Gamma)
is a natural transformation
A\overset{\mathchar 58\relax}{\to}B
,
thus a family of functions
f:(\gamma:\Gamma){\to}A\,\gamma{\to}B\,\gamma
since
\Gamma
is discrete.

Example 2.6 (
\Cat
as a NatMod
DO
).

The NatMod
DO
structure on
\cSet
extends to the
1
-category
\Cat
, substituting all instances of a discrete category
\operatorname{discr}\Gamma
by an arbitrary one
\Gamma\mathrel{:}\Cat
.
Context extension is given by the Grothendieck construction
5
5

5

The
Grothendieck construction
\int_{\Gamma}A
we consider here for

A:\Gamma{\to}\cSet
yield an
opfibration

\pi_{\Gamma}:\int_{\Gamma}A\to\Gamma
and not a fibration. In
this particular instance, the Grothendieck construction is is simply a
category of elements.

Jacobs, 2001
, 1.10.1
of a type

A\mathrel{:}\Gamma\to\cSet
and the naturality condition on adapters

\Gamma\vdash f\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow B
between
A,B\mathrel{:}\Gamma\to\cSet
becomes
non-trivial.

Example 2.7 (Full NatMod
DO
).

In a NatMod
DO
, an adapter
a
from
A
to
B
induces a term

\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}A\vdash\mathtt{0}\langle\mathord{a}[\mathord{\uparrow}]\rangle\mathrel{:}\mathord{B}[\mathord{\uparrow}]
, and this
preserves the categorical structure given by the identity adapter and adapter
composition.
A
full
NatMod
DO
is one where this embedding is an isomorphism,

i.e.
any open term induces an adapter.
Any natural model
C
can be equipped with a full NatMod
DO
structure by taking

\Gamma\vdash f\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow B\coloneq\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}A\vdash f\mathrel{:}\mathord{B}[\mathord{\uparrow}]
and
t\langle f\rangle\coloneq\mathord{f}[\symup{id}_{\Gamma}\mathbin{\triangleright}t]
.
The identity adapter is given by the variable
\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{tm}}
, and composition by substitution.

Examples

2.4
and
2.7
relate to the two well-known ways to turn a natural model into a comprehension category
Jacobs, 1993
;
Coraglia and Emmenegger, 2024a
, either discrete or full, by making either no or all terms into arrows in the category of types.

Examples

2.5
and
2.6
are instances of the latter construction.

Example 2.8 (Subtyping).

Coercive subtyping can be expressed with adapters:
MLTT
coe

Laurent et al., 2024
is an instance of NatMod
DO
where an adapter
\Gamma\vdash f\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow B
is a witness that
A
is a subtype of
B
.
Since there is at most one subtyping witness between two types, this NatMod
DO
validates the following “adapter irrelevance” rule, meaning that the category of types is a mere poset

\frac{\Gamma\vdash f\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow B\quad\Gamma\vdash f^{\prime}\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow B}{\Gamma\vdash f\equiv f^{\prime}\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow B}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=AdIrrel,right=}

For an adapter
\Gamma\vdash f\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow B
, the cast
a\langle f\rangle

is given by the coercion operation
\coeop_{A,B}a
.

Example 2.9 (TT
obs
as a NatMod
DO
).

Observational Type Theory (TT
obs
)
Altenkirch et al., 2007
is a type theory where
the operation
\operatorname{cast}{}\mathrel{:}A\sim_{\mathcal{U}}B\to A\to B
associated
to the identity type
A\sim_{\mathcal{U}}B
computes structurally.

\AtNextCite
Pujet and Tabareau (2023)
version of TT
obs
is a NatMod
DO
instance, by taking:

\frac{\Gamma\vdash e\mathrel{:}A\sim_{\mathcal{U}}B}{\Gamma\vdash\underline{e}\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow B}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=EqAd,right=}\frac{\Gamma\vdash e\mathrel{:}A\sim_{\mathcal{U}}B\quad\Gamma\vdash a\mathrel{:}A}{\Gamma\vdash a\langle\underline{e}\rangle\equiv\operatorname{cast}{}\,e\,a\mathrel{:}B}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=AdEq,right=}\frac{\Gamma\vdash A}{\Gamma\vdash\underline{\symsf{refl}}\equiv\symup{id}_{A}\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow A}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=EqReflId,right=}

By Rule
Example 2.9 (TT
obs
as a NatMod
DO
).
, any proof of observational equality between types entails
an adapter with action on term given by
\operatorname{cast}{}
(
Example 2.9 (TT
obs
as a NatMod
DO
).
).
Casts along reflexivity proofs are identities by Rule
Example 2.9 (TT
obs
as a NatMod
DO
).
, and

Pujet et al. (2025)
show that adding this equation preserves decidability of conversion.
6
6

6

Thanks to proof irrelevance, Rule
Example 2.9 (TT
obs
as a NatMod
DO
).
implies
the stronger rule where
\symsf{refl}
is replaced by an arbitrary proof of

A\sim A
, because such a proof is always convertible to
\symsf{refl}
.

It is tempting to add a similar rule for composition
(where
\operatorname{trans}
is transitivity of equality)

\frac{\Gamma\vdash e\mathrel{:}A\sim_{\mathcal{U}}B\quad\Gamma\vdash e^{\prime}\mathrel{:}B\sim_{\mathcal{U}}C}{\Gamma\vdash\underline{e}\circ\underline{e^{\prime}}\equiv\underline{\operatorname{trans}e~e^{\prime}}\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow C}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=EqCompTrans,right=}

However,
Laurent et al. (2024, Appendix A)
show that, using
this rule, we can construct a model of pure λ-calculus in the system, by working
in a context with a hypothesis
e\mathrel{:}A=A\to A
.
To avoid this obstacle to decidability of conversion,
we can instead remove this equation and instead interpret composition freely: adapters are chains of
equalities
\underline{e_{1}}\circ\ldots\circ\underline{e_{n}}
.

Example 2.10 (CastCIC as a NatMod
DO
).

CastCIC
Lennon-Bertrand et al., 2022
has been proposed as a foundation for gradual dependent types.
In CastCIC,
any
two types
A,B
are related by an adapter

\Gamma\vdash(A\Rightarrow B)\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow B
.
Non-diagonal casts such as
0\langle\symsf{Nat}\Rightarrow\symsf{Bool}\rangle
reduce
to runtime errors following the exceptional type theory of
Pédrot and Tabareau (2018)
.

\AtNextCite
Lennon-Bertrand et al. (2022)
fire triangle,
which shows that normalization cannot hold in a system satisfying the gradual guarantees, is another avatar of the obstruction exposed by
Laurent et al.
regarding the above rule for transitivity.

2.3.
Comparison with Generalized Categories with Families (gCwF)

NatMod
DO
, our model of AdapTT, is closely related to the generalized categories with families (gCwF) of
Coraglia and Emmenegger (2024b)
.
gCwF, initially introduced under the name of “plain dependent type theories”
Coraglia and Liberti, 2024
, were used by
Coraglia and Emmenegger (2024a)
to establish a connection between two formalisms to model type theory: categories with families/natural models, and comprehension categories.
The same authors then remarked
Coraglia and Emmenegger, 2024b
that, despite not being their original purpose, gCwF support some flavor of subtyping, without explicitly deriving a type cast operation.

Najmaei et al. (2026)
propose a sound syntax to work in comprehension
categories, and further the analysis of subtyping, deriving type casts for
\Pi,\Sigma
and
\Id
types.
Clarifying further the status of type casts, we establish a correspondence between gCwFs and NatMod
DO
.

Let us start with a review of
\AtNextCite
Coraglia and Emmenegger (2024b)
notations and terminology.
Natural models can be reformulated as the following diagram
Uemura, 2021
, 3.1.11
where
u
and
\dot{u}
are discrete fibrations, and
\Sigma
is a fibration
morphism with right adjoint
\Delta
(which is
not
a fibration morphism)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dot{\mathcal{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\top}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dot{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

The base category
\mathcal{B}
should be understood as that of contexts and substitutions, while
\mathcal{U}
is the category of types in contexts, and
\dot{\mathcal{U}}
that of typed terms in a context. The functors
u
and
\dot{u}
project the underlying context of a type and a term. The discrete fibration property of
u
and
\dot{u}
gives the action of substitutions on types and terms. gCwF generalize the above, merely requiring
u
and
\dot{u}
to be fibration, without dicreteness.

Dropping the discreteness condition is relevant to model type casts. Indeed, considering an object
\Gamma
of
\mathcal{B}
, and two objects
A,B
of
\mathcal{U}
in the fiber over
\Gamma
(
i.e.
two types in a context
\Gamma
), it allows for non-trivial
vertical morphisms

f:A\to B
in
\mathcal{U}
, those such that
u(f)=\symup{id}_{\Gamma}
. These play precisely the role devoted to adapters in a NatMod
DO
. However, it also relaxes the structure too much to interpret
AdapTT, as it makes the action of morphisms of
\mathcal{B}
onto the fibers in
\mathcal{U}
pseudo-functorial instead of functorial. In particular, the equations
\mathord{A}[\sigma\circ\tau]\equiv\mathord{\mathord{A}[\sigma]}[\tau]
and
\mathord{A}[\symup{id}]\equiv A
and their counterparts on terms are not valid in a gCwF. To account for this, we restrict our attention to
a subclass of gCwF that we call
split
gCwF.

Definition 2.11.

A
split
gCwF equips
u
and
\dot{u}
as above with a functorial choice of lifts, preserved by
\Sigma
and
\Delta
, such that the unit and counit are componentwise the chosen lift of their projection.

To model coercive subtyping with gCwF,
Coraglia and Emmenegger (2024b)
define an additional judgment
\Gamma\vdash a\mathrel{:}_{f}B
interpreted as the existence of a term
a
of type
A
in context
\Gamma
together with a vertical morphism
f:A\to B
. But they do not derive a proper type cast corresponding to Rule
2Judgments and rules for AdapTT (excerpt, see )
: assuming this data,
we need to construct an element
t\langle a\rangle
of
\dot{\mathcal{U}}
such that
\Sigma(t\langle a\rangle)=B
. The following proposition, proven in
Appendix

B
, resolves this discrepancy.

Proposition 2.12.

Split gCwF support a cast operation: the functor
\Sigma
induces a discrete opfibration between
\Cat
-valued presheaves, from the presheaf of fibers of
\dot{u}
to the presheaf of fibers of
u
.

Theorem 2.13.

Split
gCwF
s are equivalent to NatMod
DO
s.

Proof sketch.

The proof closely parallels the correspondence between discrete gCwF and natural models, while additionally accounting for adapters. We briefly spell out the correspondence here and refer to
Appendix

B
for the complete proof.

From a split gCwF, we define a NatMod
DO
with
\mathcal{B}
as the category of contexts.
The categories of types and terms in a context
\Gamma
are respectively given by the fiber of
u
and
\dot{u}
over
\Gamma
.
The morphism of fibrations
\Sigma
induces the natural transformation
p\mathrel{:}\Tm\overset{\mathchar 58\relax}{\to}{}\Ty
.
Its right adjoint
\Delta
then gives the context extension operation.
Finally
Proposition

2.12
shows that
p
is a discrete opfibration.

Conversely, from a NatMod
DO
, we define a split gCwF by defining the base category
\mathcal{B}=\Ctx
, the split fibrations
u\mathrel{:}\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{B}
and

\dot{u}\mathrel{:}\dot{\mathcal{U}}\to\mathcal{B}
are obtained respectively as the Grothendieck construction of the functor
\Ty\mathrel{:}\Ctx^{\op}\to\Cat
, and of the functor
\Tm\mathrel{:}\Ctx^{\op}\to\Cat
.
The natural transformation
p:\Tm\overset{\mathchar 58\relax}{\to}{}\Ty
then induces a morphism of fibrations
\Sigma\mathrel{:}\dot{\mathcal{U}}\to\mathcal{U}
. The right adjoint
\Delta
to
\Sigma
is given by the context extension operation and the action of adapters on terms.
∎

3.
Representing type formers

AdapTT, as introduced in the previous section, provides a judgmental structure
to capture type casts.
As it stands, however, the type theory does not give good tools to uniformly
describe functorial type formers.
Indeed, to be able to talk about functoriality, we need to have categories
between which a type former maps. We have just worked to make the codomain clear: it is the category
of types and adapters. However, the domain can be much more complex: as shown
by
Example

2.1
, it can involve multiple types, dependency,
and variance information.

In the standard setting, the description of type formers’ domain typically relies on
universes. In
Agda
, for instance, the type former for lists would be declared by:

data

\List
(
X
:
Set
) :
Set

The universe
Set
provides
a (partial) internalization of the judgment for well-formed types.
In AdapTT, however, this will not do. As adapters are a type-level notion only,
and terms form a mere set, universes in AdapTT could internalize types, but not their categorical structure,
and are thus ill-suited to describe the category that is the domain of a type former.

To address this, we extend AdapTT to a theory AdapTT
2
with a primitive notion of type variables.
AdapTT
2
will be used to satisfactorily describe the parameters of inductive types, such as
\List
above, in
Section

4
.
We start in
Section

3.1
by distilling how these type variables ought to work,
to explain the design of AdapTT
2
.
Then,
Section

3.2
formally describes the type theory per se,
while
Section

3.3
builds a model of it on top of a model of AdapTT,
providing a formal construction of functorial type formers for AdapTT out of
AdapTT
2
’s ones.

3.1.
What do we want of type variables?

In this section, we motivate our choice of considering type variables
and the form of the rules we provide for them by looking at examples and
expected features. For the sake of presentation, we use an informal,
named syntax with implicit weakenings. We switch
back to a formal, name-free version in
Section

3.2
. Moreover, none
of the rules presented here are defining a theory, but rather they constitute
desiderata for rules that must be derivable in our proposed theory. For this
reason some are left unnamed, and many are presented in a simplified
variant.

3.1.1.
Lists: type variables and transformations.

As we see for
\List
above, the input of a type former
is specified by a context. In that case, this context
consists of a single type variable:
\Gamma_{\List}\coloneq(X\mathrel{:}\Ty)
.
This is sufficient to capture the
\List
type former, given by Rule
3.1.1.
Lists: type variables and transformations.

below. Indeed, by the general Rule
6Type variables
,
Rule
3.1.1.
Lists: type variables and transformations.
is derivable. Moreover,
a substitution
\Delta\vdash\sigma\mathrel{:}\Gamma_{\List}
consists
of a single type:
\sigma\equiv\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathbin{\triangleright}\mathord{X}[\sigma]
.
We hence obtain the more standard
Rule
3.1.1.
Lists: type variables and transformations.
. Conversely, instantiating Rule
3.1.1.
Lists: type variables and transformations.

with
\symup{id}_{\Gamma_{\List}}
or Rule
3.1.1.
Lists: type variables and transformations.
with
(X:\Ty)\vdash X
,
we recover Rule
3.1.1.
Lists: type variables and transformations.
.
7
7

7

For the category theorist, this is a manifestation, on objects,
of the (2-categorical) Yoneda lemma,
which says that
\Ty(\Gamma)

is isomorphic to the category of natural transformations

\Sub(\cdot,\Gamma)\to\Ty
.

As all three versions are equivalent, we favor the less verbose
style of Rule
3.1.1.
Lists: type variables and transformations.
.

\Gamma_{\List}\coloneq(X\mathrel{:}\Ty)\frac{}{\Gamma_{\List}\vdash\List}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=ListUniv,right=}\par\frac{\Delta\vdash\sigma\mathrel{:}\Gamma_{\List}}{\Delta\vdash\mathord{\List}[\sigma]}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=ListSubs,right=}\par\frac{\Delta\vdash A}{\Delta\vdash\List(A)}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=ListTy,right=}

Type variables are given by a context extension operation
\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}
,
given by the rules below.
Any well-formed context can be extended with a type variable, which can then
be accessed to yield a type.
A substitution into an extension
\Gamma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(X\mathrel{:}\Ty)

is built out of a substitution into
\Gamma
together with a
well-formed type instantiating the type variable.

\frac{\vdash\Gamma}{\vdash\Gamma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(X\mathrel{:}\Ty)}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}\frac{\vdash\Gamma}{\Gamma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(X\mathrel{:}\Ty)\vdash X}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}\frac{\Gamma\vdash A}{\Gamma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(X\mathrel{:}\Ty)\vdash A}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}\frac{\Gamma\vdash\sigma\mathrel{:}\Delta\quad\Gamma\vdash A}{\Gamma\vdash\sigma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}A\mathrel{:}\Delta\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(X\mathrel{:}\Ty)}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}

But there is more happening.
Indeed, consider two substitutions

\Delta\vdash\sigma,\tau\mathrel{:}\Gamma_{\List}
,
amounting to two types
\mathord{X}[\sigma],\mathord{X}[\tau]
.
We have an interesting way to relate these: adapters!
Hence, in AdapTT
2
, there is a natural
and interesting way to relate substitutions, which
we call
transformations
, after
Licata and Harper (2011)
.
Transformations are typed by a judgment

\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathrel{:}\sigma\Rightarrow_{\Delta}\tau
, where

\Gamma\vdash\sigma,\tau\mathrel{:}\Delta
, and
collect adapters between their endpoints.
That is, to create a transformation relating
substitutions
\sigma,\tau
respectively extended by types
A
and

B
, we must provide a transformation
\mu\mathrel{:}\sigma\Rightarrow\tau

and an adapter
f\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow B
.

\frac{\Gamma\vdash\sigma,\tau\mathrel{:}\Delta\quad\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathrel{:}\sigma\Rightarrow_{\Delta}\tau\quad\Gamma\vdash A,B\quad\Gamma\vdash f\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow B}{\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}f\mathrel{:}\sigma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}A\Rightarrow_{\Delta\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(X:\Ty)}\tau\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}B}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}

In categorical terms,
\Ctx
in AdapTT
2
is a
2-category
,
whose objects are contexts, 1-morphisms substitutions,
and 2-morphisms the transformations just introduced.

\Ty
also interacts with this structure: transformations act on types.
Indeed, the core idea of AdapTT
2
is that a transformation
\mu
acting
on a type
A
induces an adapter:

\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathrel{:}\sigma\Rightarrow_{\Delta}\tau\quad\Delta\vdash A}{\Gamma\vdash\mathord{A}\llbracket\mu\rrbracket\mathrel{:}\mathord{A}[\sigma]\Rightarrow\mathord{A}[\tau]}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=TransTy,right=}

AdapTT
2
internalizes the functoriality of types:
any type
A
in
\Delta
, given a context
\Gamma
,
gives rise to a functor from the category of substitutions from
\Gamma

to
\Delta
to that of types in
\Gamma
,
and Rule
3.1.1.
Lists: type variables and transformations.
corresponds to the action on morphisms (transformations).
This gives us a way to
compute
the source category of
A

as a functor, by analyzing the structure of its context
\Delta
.
Thus, the more expressive the contexts,
the more interesting functorial type formers we can capture.

For instance, for lists, we derive Rule
3.1.1.
Lists: type variables and transformations.
below. Specializing it
as we did above, we obtain Rule
3.1.1.
Lists: type variables and transformations.
,
corresponding to the usual subtyping rule for lists, or to a kind of map operation.

\frac{\Delta\vdash\sigma,\tau\mathrel{:}\Gamma_{\List}\quad\Delta\vdash\mu\mathrel{:}\sigma\Rightarrow_{\Gamma_{\List}}\tau}{\Delta\vdash\mathord{\List}\llbracket\mu\rrbracket\mathrel{:}\mathord{\List}[\sigma]\Rightarrow\mathord{\List}[\tau]}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=TransList,right=}\frac{\Delta\vdash A,B\quad\Delta\vdash f\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow B}{\Delta\vdash\List(f)\mathrel{:}\List(A)\Rightarrow\List(B)}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=AdList,right=}

As a more involved example, combining the formation rule for lists above and
that for sums below,
by a single application of Rule
3.1.1.
Lists: type variables and transformations.
,
we can derive functoriality for a combination of them.

\frac{}{(X\mathrel{:}\Ty)\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(Y\mathrel{:}\Ty)\vdash X+Y}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}\frac{\Delta\vdash A,A^{\prime},B,B^{\prime}\quad\Delta\vdash a\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow A^{\prime}\quad\Delta\vdash b\mathrel{:}B\Rightarrow B^{\prime}}{\Delta\vdash\mathord{(\List(X+\List(Y)))}\llbracket a\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}b\rrbracket\mathrel{:}\List(A+\List(B))\Rightarrow\List(A^{\prime}+\List(B^{\prime}))}

The behavior of this complex adapter is obtained by
following the type’s structure,
i.e.
by combining
the functoriality of the
\List
and
+
types.
In general, in AdapTT
2
, the functoriality of complex types
is derived compositionally from that of each type former.

3.1.2.
Identity: transformations and terms

So far, we omitted to consider terms. However, just like types, terms should be acted upon
by transformations, and conversely we should have a way to extend transformations
to account for term variables. Here, the insight is that since we have only a set of
terms, the only way to relate them is by a definitional equality constraint, as follows:

\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathrel{:}\sigma\Rightarrow_{\Delta}\tau\quad\Delta\vdash A\quad\Gamma\vdash t\mathrel{:}\mathord{A}[\sigma]\quad\Gamma\vdash u\mathrel{:}\mathord{A}[\tau]\quad\Gamma\vdash t\langle\mathord{A}\llbracket\mu\rrbracket\rangle\equiv u\mathrel{:}\mathord{A}[\tau]}{\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathbin{\triangleright}t\mathrel{:}(\sigma\mathbin{\triangleright}t)\Rightarrow_{\Delta\mathbin{\triangleright}(x\mathrel{:}A)}(\tau\mathbin{\triangleright}u)}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=TransTm,right=}\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathrel{:}\sigma\Rightarrow_{\Delta}\tau\quad\Delta\vdash A\quad\Delta\vdash t\mathrel{:}A}{\Gamma\vdash\mathord{t}[\sigma]\langle\mathord{A}\llbracket\mu\rrbracket\rangle\equiv\mathord{t}[\tau]\mathrel{:}\mathord{A}[\tau]}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=TransTyAdTm,right=}

In particular, the equality constraint required in Rule
3.1.2.
Identity: transformations and terms

can be exactly recovered by specializing Rule
3.1.2.
Identity: transformations and terms

to
t\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{tm}}
, so the rules are in harmony.

An interesting example that uses term variables is the identity type, given by

\Gamma_{\Id}\coloneq(X\mathrel{:}\Ty)\mathbin{\triangleright}(x\mathrel{:}X)\mathbin{\triangleright}(y\mathrel{:}X)

We can compute that a substitution
\Delta\vdash\sigma\mathrel{:}\Gamma_{\Id}

consists of a type
\Delta\vdash\mathord{X}[\sigma]

and two of its inhabitants, while transformations between
these correspond to an adapter between the types preserving the terms.
In other words, we obtain the following rule:

\frac{\Gamma\vdash A,B\quad\Gamma\vdash a,a^{\prime}\mathrel{:}A\quad\Gamma\vdash f\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow B}{\Gamma\vdash\mathord{\Id}\llbracket f\mathbin{\triangleright}a\mathbin{\triangleright}a^{\prime}\rrbracket\mathrel{:}\mathord{\Id}[A\mathbin{\triangleright}a\mathbin{\triangleright}a^{\prime}]\Rightarrow\mathord{\Id}[B\mathbin{\triangleright}a\langle f\rangle\mathbin{\triangleright}a^{\prime}\langle f\rangle]}

We recover the complex-looking typing rule of MLTT
map

Laurent et al., 2024
,
completely mechanically, simply from the description
\Gamma_{\Id}
.
Furthermore, the computation rule we derive for it in
Section

4.2
is

(\symsf{refl}~a)\langle\mathord{\Id}\llbracket f\mathbin{\triangleright}a\mathbin{\triangleright}a\rrbracket\rangle\equiv\symsf{refl}~(a\langle f\rangle)

which also agrees with the one of MLTT
map
.

3.1.3.
Functions and trees: contravariance

Type variables as just introduced are not
quite enough to adequately capture all interesting examples.
First, as seen in the case of
\P
, we need a form of contravariance:
adapters for the domain need to go in the direction
opposite to that of the codomain.
To describe this, we annotate type variables in contexts
with a
direction

\Dir\coloneq+\mid-
.

\frac{\vdash\Gamma}{\vdash\Gamma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(X\mathrel{:}\Ty_{d})}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=($d:\Dir$)}\frac{\vdash\Gamma}{\Gamma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(X\mathrel{:}\Ty_{+})\vdash X}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}

Type variables from
Section

3.1.1
correspond to the
+
direction.
Crucially, we still only allow access to covariant variables:
since a type
\Gamma\vdash A
is implicitly always covariant with
respect to its context, it does not make sense to directly access contravariant
variables.

To nonetheless be able to use contravariant variables,
and more generally describe contravariant
types, we introduce an operation on contexts called
dualization
,
written
\cdot^{-}
. It reverses
variables’ direction,
e.g.

((X\mathrel{:}\Ty_{-})\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(Y\mathrel{:}\Ty_{+}))^{-}\equiv(X\mathrel{:}\Ty_{+})\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(Y\mathrel{:}\Ty_{-})
.
Combined with the rule for (covariant) variable,
this lets us
e.g.
derive

((X\mathrel{:}\Ty_{-})\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(Y\mathrel{:}\Ty_{+}))^{-}\vdash X
.
A contravariant type in
\Gamma
is then simply a type in
\Gamma^{-}
.
Equipped with dualization, we extend the rules for substitutions and transformations:
we can substitute a contravariant type for a contravariant type variable but,
more interestingly, the direction of adapters for a contravariant
type variable is
reversed
. That is, to construct a transformation

\mu\mathrel{:}\sigma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}A\Rightarrow_{\Delta\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(X:\Ty_{-})}\tau\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}B
, we must provide an
adapter
f\mathrel{:}B\Rightarrow A
.

\frac{\Delta\vdash\sigma\mathrel{:}\Gamma\quad\Delta^{-}\vdash A}{\Delta\vdash\sigma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}A\mathrel{:}\Gamma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(X\mathrel{:}\Ty_{-})}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathrel{:}\sigma\Rightarrow_{\Delta}\tau\quad\Gamma^{-}\vdash A,B\quad\Gamma^{-}\vdash f\mathrel{:}B\Rightarrow A}{\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}f\mathrel{:}\sigma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}A\Rightarrow_{\Delta\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(X:\Ty_{-})}\tau\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}B}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}

We also allow term variables of contravariant type, although as for type
variables we only allow access to those of covariant type, the other
must be accessed via dualization.

\frac{\Gamma^{-}\vdash A}{\vdash\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}_{-}(x\mathrel{:}A)}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}

This lets us construct the context

\Gamma_{\to}\coloneq(X\mathrel{:}\Ty_{-})\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(Y\mathrel{:}\Ty_{+})
of the non-dependent
function type, and derive the corresponding adapter typing rule.

\frac{\Gamma^{-}\vdash A,A^{\prime}\quad\Gamma\vdash B,B^{\prime}\quad\Gamma^{-}\vdash a\mathrel{:}A^{\prime}\Rightarrow A\quad\Gamma\vdash b\mathrel{:}B\Rightarrow B^{\prime}\quad}{\Gamma\vdash\mathord{\mathord{\to}}\llbracket a\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}b\rrbracket\mathrel{:}A\to B\Rightarrow A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}}

We can give it the computation rule

(f\langle\mathord{\mathord{\to}}\llbracket a\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}b\rrbracket\rangle)\ u\equiv(f\ u\langle a\rangle)\langle b\rangle
,
a simpler variant of
Example

2.1
.

Another example is the type
\Tree
of
Y
-branching trees
with
X
-storing nodes, with context of parameters

\Gamma_{\Tree}\coloneq(X\mathrel{:}\Ty_{+})\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(Y\mathrel{:}\Ty_{-})
and
constructors and equations as follows (where
f\mathrel{:}X\Rightarrow X^{\prime}
and

g\mathrel{:}Y\Rightarrow Y^{\prime}
)

\small\frac{}{(X:\Ty_{+})\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(Y:\Ty_{-})\vdash\mathord{\operatorname{leaf}}\mathrel{:}\Tree}\frac{}{(X:\Ty_{+})\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(Y:\Ty_{-})\mathbin{\triangleright}(x:X)\mathbin{\triangleright}(r:Y\to\Tree)\vdash\mathord{\operatorname{node}}\mathrel{:}\mathord{\Tree}[X\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}Y]}\\
\mathord{\mathord{\operatorname{leaf}}}[X\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}Y]\langle\mathord{\Tree}\llbracket f\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}g\rrbracket\rangle\equiv\mathord{\mathord{\operatorname{leaf}}}[X^{\prime}\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}Y^{\prime}]\mathord{\mathord{\operatorname{node}}}[X\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}Y\mathbin{\triangleright}x\mathbin{\triangleright}r]\langle\mathord{\Tree}\llbracket f\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}g\rrbracket\rangle\equiv\mathord{\mathord{\operatorname{node}}}[X^{\prime}\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}Y^{\prime}\mathbin{\triangleright}x\langle f\rangle\mathbin{\triangleright}r\langle\mathord{Y\to\Tree}\llbracket f\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}g\rrbracket\rangle]

By η-expansion, we can further compute that

r\langle\mathord{Y\to\Tree}\llbracket f\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}g\rrbracket\rangle\equiv\l x\mathchar 58\relax~\mathord{r}[x\langle g\rangle]\langle\mathord{\Tree}\llbracket f\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}g\rrbracket\rangle
.
Again this is an inductive type whose computation rules we can automatically derive.
As for functions, we see the need for the contravariant adapter
g

which acts by “pre-composition” for
\mathord{\operatorname{node}}
.

3.1.4.
\Sig
: dependency

So far, all our examples have been non-dependent. But if we want to capture
dependent function or pair types, the type variables as introduced for now
are not sufficient. To gain the ability to represent dependency, we further generalize them
to bind a term-level variable.
For simplicity, we allow only a single binding for now,
but generalize to telescopes of types in
Section

3.2
.
Our new rules for context extension and variables are now

\frac{\Gamma\vdash A}{\vdash\Gamma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(X:A\mathchar 58\relax\mathord{\Ty})}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}\frac{\Gamma\vdash A}{(\Gamma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(X:A\mathchar 58\relax\mathord{\Ty}))\mathbin{\triangleright}(x:A)\vdash X}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}\frac{\Gamma\vdash\sigma\mathrel{:}\Delta\quad\Delta\vdash A\quad\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}\mathord{A}[\sigma]\vdash B}{\Gamma\vdash\sigma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}B\mathrel{:}\Gamma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(X:A\mathchar 58\relax\mathord{\Ty})}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathrel{:}\sigma\Rightarrow_{\Delta}\tau\quad\Delta\vdash A\quad\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}(x:\mathord{A}[\sigma])\vdash f\mathrel{:}B\Rightarrow\mathord{B^{\prime}}[\symup{id}\mathbin{\triangleright}x\langle\mathord{A}\llbracket\mu\rrbracket\rangle]}{\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}f\mathrel{:}\sigma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}B\Rightarrow_{\Delta\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(X:A\mathchar 58\relax\mathord{\Ty})}\tau\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}B^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}

Accessing the type variables gives a type with access to an extra binder,
and accordingly in a substitution that type variable must be replaced by one
allowed to use this extra binder. Finally, transformations must be extended by an
adapter between the dependent types, suitably lying over the base transformation.
For instance, the context for the dependent pair type is

\Gamma_{\Sig}\coloneq(X:\Ty_{+})\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(Y:X\mathchar 58\relax\Ty_{+})
where

Y
depends on a term of type
X
.
Once Rule
3.1.1.
Lists: type variables and transformations.
is unfolded, its adapter is typed as follows

\frac{\Delta\vdash A,A^{\prime}\quad\Delta\vdash a\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow A^{\prime}\quad\quad\Delta\mathbin{\triangleright}(x:A)\vdash B\quad\Delta\mathbin{\triangleright}(x:A^{\prime})\vdash B^{\prime}\quad\Delta\mathbin{\triangleright}(x:A)\vdash b\mathrel{:}B\Rightarrow\mathord{B^{\prime}}[\symup{id}\mathbin{\triangleright}x\langle a\rangle]}{\Delta\vdash\mathord{\Sig}\llbracket a\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}b\rrbracket\mathrel{:}\Sig(x:A)\mathchar 58\relax B\Rightarrow\Sig(x:A^{\prime})\mathchar 58\relax B^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=AdPairEx,right=}

\W
and
\P
: putting it all together

As examples which combine both contravariance and dependency, we can look at
the type
\W

of well-founded trees, given by

\Gamma_{\W}\coloneq(X:\Ty_{+})\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(Y:X\mathchar 58\relax\Ty_{-})
,
and the one of dependent functions

\Gamma_{\P}\coloneq(X:\Ty_{-})\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(Y:X\mathchar 58\relax\Ty_{+})
,
for which we also need to allow the type of variables bound by a type variable
to be contravariant. We defer the general rules for variables for a moment,
but the derived typing rules for adapters in these examples are as follows:

\frac{\Delta\vdash A,A^{\prime}\quad\Delta\vdash a\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow A^{\prime}\quad\quad(\Delta\mathbin{\triangleright}(x:A))^{-}\vdash B\quad(\Delta\mathbin{\triangleright}(x:A^{\prime}))^{-}\vdash B^{\prime}\quad(\Delta\mathbin{\triangleright}(x:A))^{-}\vdash b\mathrel{:}\mathord{B^{\prime}}[\symup{id}\mathbin{\triangleright}x\langle a\rangle]\Rightarrow B}{\Delta\vdash\mathord{\W}\llbracket a\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}b\rrbracket\mathrel{:}\W(x:A)\mathchar 58\relax B\Rightarrow\W(x:A^{\prime})\mathchar 58\relax B^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=AdWEx,right=}\frac{\Delta^{-}\vdash A,A^{\prime}\quad\Delta^{-}\vdash a\mathrel{:}A^{\prime}\Rightarrow A\quad\quad\Delta\mathbin{\triangleright}(x:A)\vdash B\quad\Delta\mathbin{\triangleright}(x:A^{\prime})\vdash B^{\prime}\quad\Delta\mathbin{\triangleright}(x:A^{\prime})\vdash b\mathrel{:}\mathord{B}[\symup{id}\mathbin{\triangleright}x\langle a\rangle]\Rightarrow B^{\prime}}{\Delta\vdash\mathord{\P}\llbracket a\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}b\rrbracket\mathrel{:}\P(x:A)\mathchar 58\relax B\Rightarrow\P(x:A^{\prime})\mathchar 58\relax B^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=AdFunEx,right=}

As we can see, there are subtle but important discrepancies in the types
of the “dependent adapter” between
\Sig
,
\W
and
\P
, and
the judgmental structure offered by AdapTT
2
is leveraged in correctly
handling these discrepancies.

3.2.
A 2-Dimensional Type Theory with Adapters

We are now finally in the position to give the official rules of AdapTT
2
.
The main judgments are collected in
Fig.

3
.
Formally, we understand these
as describing a generalized algebraic theory (GAT)

Cartmell, 1986
;
Bezem et al., 2021
, whose sorts we also collect in

Fig.

3
. We use both interchangeably,
i.e.
write
either
\sigma\mathrel{:}\Sub(\Gamma,\Delta)
or
\Gamma\vdash\sigma\mathrel{:}\Delta
.
In premises, we often omit variables appearing in later objects, and whose
sort can be inferred.
The full rules are collected in
Appendix

D
,
and have been mostly type checked in
Agda
as
postulated symbols and equations, using rewrite rules
to emulate an extensional type theory as meta-theory.

Basic judgmental structure

\frac{}{\Ctx}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}\frac{\Gamma,\Delta\mathrel{:}\Ctx}{\Sub(\Gamma,\Delta)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma,\Delta\mathrel{:}\Ctx}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\sigma,\tau\mathrel{:}\Sub(\Gamma,\Delta)}{\Trans({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta},\sigma,\tau)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{:}\Ctx}{\Ty(\Gamma)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\mathrel{:}\Ctx}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994ptA,B\mathrel{:}\Ty(\Gamma)}{\Ad({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma},A,B)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\mathrel{:}\Ctx}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994ptA\mathrel{:}\Ty(\Gamma)}{\Tm(\Gamma,A)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}\\
\vdash\Box\Gamma\vdash\Box\mathrel{:}\Delta\Gamma\vdash\Box\mathrel{:}\sigma\Rightarrow_{\Delta}\tau\Gamma\vdash\Box\Gamma\vdash\Box\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow B\Gamma\vdash\Box\mathrel{:}A\vskip-4.62497pt\\
\rule{310.4979pt}{0.5pt}\vskip-4.62497pt\\
\frac{\Gamma\vdash\sigma\mathrel{:}\Delta}{\Gamma\vdash\symup{id}\mathrel{:}\sigma\Rightarrow_{\Delta}\sigma}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=TransId,right=}\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathrel{:}\rho\Rightarrow_{\Delta}\sigma\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash\nu\mathrel{:}\sigma\Rightarrow_{\Delta}\tau\hskip 9.24994pt}{\Gamma\vdash\nu\circ\mu\mathrel{:}\rho\Rightarrow_{\Delta}\tau}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=TransComp,right=}\\
\frac{\Delta\vdash\tau\mathrel{:}\Xi\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathrel{:}\sigma\Rightarrow_{\Delta}\sigma^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt}{\Gamma\vdash\tau\circ\mu\mathrel{:}(\tau\circ\sigma)\Rightarrow_{\Xi}(\tau\circ\sigma^{\prime})}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=TransWhiskerLeft,right=}\frac{\Delta\vdash\nu\mathrel{:}\tau\Rightarrow_{\Xi}\tau^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash\sigma\mathrel{:}\Delta\hskip 9.24994pt}{\Gamma\vdash\nu\circ\sigma\mathrel{:}(\tau\circ\sigma)\Rightarrow_{\Xi}(\tau^{\prime}\circ\sigma)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=TransWhiskerRight,right=}\\
\frac{\Delta\vdash A\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathrel{:}\sigma\Rightarrow_{\Delta}\tau\hskip 9.24994pt}{\Gamma\vdash\mathord{A}\llbracket\mu\rrbracket\mathrel{:}\mathord{A}[\sigma]\Rightarrow\mathord{A}[\tau]}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,lab=TransTy,right=}\frac{\Delta\vdash t\mathrel{:}A\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathrel{:}\sigma\Rightarrow_{\Delta}\tau}{\Gamma\vdash\mathord{t}[\sigma]\langle\mathord{A}\llbracket\mu\rrbracket\rangle\equiv\mathord{t}[\tau]\mathrel{:}\mathord{A}[\tau]}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,lab=TransTm,right=}\frac{\Delta\vdash f\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow B\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathrel{:}\sigma\Rightarrow_{\Delta}\tau}{\Gamma\vdash\mathord{B}\llbracket\mu\rrbracket\circ\mathord{f}[\sigma]\equiv\mathord{f}[\tau]\circ\mathord{A}\llbracket\mu\rrbracket\mathrel{:}\mathord{A}[\sigma]\Rightarrow\mathord{B}[\tau]}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,lab=TransAd,right=}\\
\frac{\vdash\Gamma}{\vdash\Gamma^{-}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=CtxDual,right=}\frac{\Gamma\vdash\sigma\mathrel{:}\Delta}{\Gamma^{-}\vdash\sigma^{-}\mathrel{:}\Delta^{-}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=SubDual,right=}\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathrel{:}\sigma\Rightarrow_{\Delta}\tau\hskip 9.24994pt}{\Gamma^{-}\vdash\mu^{-}\mathrel{:}\tau^{-}\Rightarrow_{\Delta^{-}}\sigma^{-}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=TransDual,right=}

Figure 3.
Basic structure of AdapTT
2
(excerpt)
Basic judgment forms of \AdapTTt(those of \AdapTT, plus a new one for transformations),
rules for transformations (they form a category, and
act on terms and types), and rules for duality, which acts on substitutions
and transformations.

The core judgments of AdapTT
2
, given on top of
Fig.

3
,
extend those of AdapTT with
transformations, which relates two substitutions.
We also use the group of directions
\Dir
with elements
+
and
-
,
isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
.

The base operations of AdapTT
2
are collected in the rest of
Fig.

3
.
We omit the empty context
\mathord{\diamondsuit}

and unique substitution
\Gamma\vdash\mathord{\diamondsuit}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{:}\mathord{\diamondsuit}
, similar
to AdapTT.
We can compose transformations,
and left and right whisker a
transformation by a substitution, which respectively generalize the
composition of transformations,
action of transformations on terms/types, and of substitution on adapters.
And indeed, the former compute like componentwise versions of the latter.
Categorically, these operations and their equations amount to saying that

\Ctx
is a 2-category with
\Sub
and
\Trans
respectively as 1- and 2-morphisms.

As in AdapTT,
\Ty(\Gamma)
also forms a category (with
\Ad
as morphisms),
but the novelty is that these categories assemble in a
2
-functor
\Ty
.
That is, given
\sigma\mathrel{:}\Sub(\Gamma,\Delta)
, substitution

\mathord{\cdot}[\sigma]
is a functor between
\Ty(\Delta)
and
\Ty(\Gamma)

(as in AdapTT), but moreover
\mathord{\cdot}\llbracket\mu\rrbracket
is a natural transformation
between the functors corresponding to its endpoints.
Similarly,
\Tm
is a “dependent 2-functor”,
albeit a degenerate one:
the action of a transformation on a term is a definitional equality
3Basic structure of AdapTT2 (excerpt)
.

Finally, we have the dualization operation
\cdot^{-}
,
which lets us represent objects that depend contravariantly on the context.
Dualization also acts on substitutions and transformations, essentially as the identity,
although it formally reverses the direction of the latter.
The empty context and substitutions are mapped to themselves:

\mathord{\diamondsuit}^{-}\equiv\mathord{\diamondsuit}
and

(\mathord{\diamondsuit}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma^{-}}})^{-}\equiv\mathord{\diamondsuit}_{\Gamma}
.
Given a direction
d\mathrel{:}\Dir
, we write
(\cdot)^{d}

with the convention that
(\cdot)^{-}
is the one of
Fig.

3

and
(\cdot)^{+}
is the identity. As
(\cdot)^{dd^{\prime}}
coincides with

((\cdot)^{d})^{d^{\prime}}
, this defines a group action of directions on contexts. Since directions are a meta-level set,
a rule involving direction variables should
be formally understood as a family of rules, one for each possible
concrete combination of directions.

\frac{\vdash\Gamma\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma^{d}\vdash A}{\vdash\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}}A}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,lab=CtxExtTm,right=($d:\Dir$)}\frac{\Gamma\vdash\sigma\mathrel{:}\Delta\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta^{d}\vdash A}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma^{d}\vdash t\mathrel{:}\mathord{A}[\sigma^{d}]}{\Gamma\vdash\sigma\mathbin{\triangleright}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}}t\mathrel{:}\Delta\mathbin{\triangleright}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}}A}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,lab=SubExtTm,right=($d:\Dir$)}\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathrel{:}\sigma\Rightarrow_{\Delta}\tau\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash t\mathrel{:}\mathord{A}[\sigma]}{\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathbin{\triangleright}_{+}t\mathrel{:}\sigma\mathbin{\triangleright}_{+}t\Rightarrow\tau\mathbin{\triangleright}_{+}t{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\langle\mathord{A}\llbracket\mu\rrbracket\rangle}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,lab=TransTm+,right=}\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathrel{:}\sigma\Rightarrow_{\Delta}\tau\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma^{-}\vdash t\mathrel{:}\mathord{A}[\tau^{-}]}{\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathbin{\triangleright}_{-}t\mathrel{:}\sigma\mathbin{\triangleright}_{-}t{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\langle\mathord{A}\llbracket\mu^{-}\rrbracket\rangle}\Rightarrow\tau\mathbin{\triangleright}_{-}t}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,lab=TransTm-,right=}\frac{\vdash\Gamma\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma^{d}\vdash A}{\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}}A\vdash\mathord{\uparrow}_{A}\mathrel{:}\Gamma}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,lab=WkTm,right=($d:\Dir$)}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash\Gamma}\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash A}}{\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}_{+}A\vdash\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{tm}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A}}\mathrel{:}\mathord{A}[\mathord{\uparrow}]}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,lab=VarZTm,right=}\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathrel{:}\sigma\Rightarrow_{\Delta}\tau\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash Contexts can be extended by term variables, and substitutions and
transformations can accordingly be extended by terms. For the latter, a term
corresponds to a definitional constraint between the substitutions.

Term variables

Term variables (
Fig.

4
)
behave broadly similarly to those of AdapTT, with two differences.
The first is the ability to bind variables of contravariant type, a
flexibility used to represent
e.g.

-types, as explained in
Section

.
Note, however, that we only allow accessing covariant variables in Rule

from Rule
4Term variables in AdapTT2 (equations for

and

and the equation

The second is the extension of transformations, which we touched upon in

Dualization acts on the primitives of

For instance, we have

Rules for telescopes (excerpt) –
Note that telescopes only contain term variables
Judgments for telescopes (well-formed telescope, telescope adapter,
