Telescopes

In
Section

3.1
, we saw the need for
dependent type variables.
Our basic examples only need 0 or 1 binder, but to allow for a uniform treatment of these
and more complex examples with more binders, we introduce telescopes,
whose main rules are collected in
Fig.

5
. Telescopes
represent context extensions, although limited to term variables only.
Hence, they are lists of types (in a pre-existing context), and
informally behave as iterated
\Sig
-types.
Telescopes in context
\Gamma
are built out of the empty telescope
\mathord{\diamondsuit}
by successive telescope extension
\Theta\mathbin{\triangleright}A
, which extends
\Theta
with
a new term variable of type
A
.
A context
\Gamma
can be extended by a telescope
\Theta
defined over it:

\displaystyle\vdash\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}_{d}\mathord{\diamondsuit}\equiv

\displaystyle~\Gamma

\displaystyle\vdash\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}_{d}(\Theta\mathbin{\triangleright}A)\equiv(\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}_{d}\Theta)\mathbin{\triangleright}_{d}A

All types in a telescope have the same (covariant) direction. Just
like for types, we can represent a contravariant telescope
\Theta
in
\Gamma

(where all types are contravariant) as an element of
\Tel(\Gamma^{-})
.

Telescopes come with their own sort of adapters
\TelAd
, which are lists of
componentwise adapters between two telescopes, and instantiations
\Inst
,
which are lists of terms inhabiting the respective types of a telescope.
The behavior of telescopes, instantiations and telescope adapters
mirrors that of respectively types, terms and adapters,
so we reuse the notations for the judgments of the latter.
They come with a somewhat tedious although unsurprising calculus,
where all operations are componentwise.
We record only the most interesting ones: the
telescope weakening
\mathord{\uparrow}_{\Theta}
, obtained by composing the individual
weakenings for the types in
\Theta
,
i.e.

\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}_{d}(\Theta\mathbin{\triangleright}A)\vdash\mathord{\uparrow}_{\Theta\mathbin{\triangleright}A}\equiv\mathord{\uparrow}_{\Theta}\circ\mathord{\uparrow}_{A}\mathrel{:}\Gamma

and the variable instantiation
\vinst
, which consists
of a list of variables, in that

\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}\Theta\mathbin{\triangleright}A\vdash\vinst_{\Theta\mathbin{\triangleright}A}\equiv\mathord{\vinst_{\Theta}}[\mathord{\uparrow}_{A}]\mathbin{\triangleright}\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{tm}}\mathrel{:}\mathord{(\Theta\mathbin{\triangleright}A)}[\mathord{\uparrow}]

This instantiation plays a role very similar to that of
\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{tm}}
.
Finally, just as for terms we can define the functorial action
\mathbin{\mathord{\triangleright}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\triangleright}}

of context extension by a telescope:

\sigma\mathbin{\mathord{\triangleright}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\triangleright}}\alpha\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}(\sigma\circ\mathord{\uparrow})\mathbin{\triangleright}\vinst\langle\mathord{\alpha}[\mathord{\uparrow}]\rangle
.

\frac{\vdash\Gamma\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma^{d^{\prime}}\vdash\Theta}{\vdash{\Gamma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{d^{\prime}}(\Theta\mathchar 58\relax\mathord{\Ty}_{d})}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=CtxTy,right=($d,d^{\prime}:\Dir$)}\frac{\Gamma\vdash\sigma\mathrel{:}\Delta\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta^{d^{\prime}}\vdash\Theta\hskip 9.24994pt\left(\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}_{d^{\prime}}\mathord{\Theta}[\sigma^{d^{\prime}}]\right)^{d}\vdash A}{\Gamma\vdash\sigma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{d}A\mathrel{:}\Delta\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{d^{\prime}}(\Theta\mathchar 58\relax\mathord{\Ty}_{d})}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=SubTy,right=($d,d^{\prime}:\Dir$)}\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathrel{:}\sigma\Rightarrow_{\Delta}\tau\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta\vdash\Theta\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}_{+}\Theta{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[\sigma]}\vdash A\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}_{+}\Theta{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[\tau]}\vdash B\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}_{+}\Theta{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[\sigma]}\vdash f\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow B{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[\symup{id}_{\Gamma}\mathbin{\mathord{\triangleright}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\triangleright}}_{+}\mathord{\Theta}\llbracket\mu\rrbracket]}}{\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{+}f\mathrel{:}\sigma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{+}A\Rightarrow_{\Delta\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{+}(\Theta\mathchar 58\relax\mathord{\Ty}_{+})}\tau\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{+}B}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=TransAd++,right=}\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathrel{:}\sigma\Rightarrow_{\Delta}\tau\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta\vdash\Theta\hskip 9.24994pt\left(\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}_{+}\Theta{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[\sigma]}\right)^{-}\vdash A\hskip 9.24994pt\left(\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}_{+}\Theta{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[\tau]}\right)^{-}\vdash B\hskip 9.24994pt\left(\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}_{+}\Theta{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[\sigma]}\right)^{-}\vdash f\mathrel{:}B{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[\symup{id}_{\Gamma}\mathbin{\mathord{\triangleright}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\triangleright}}_{+}\mathord{\Theta}\llbracket\mu\rrbracket]}\Rightarrow A}{\Gamma\vdash\mu\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{-}f\mathrel{:}\sigma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{-}A\Rightarrow_{\Delta\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{+}(\Theta\mathchar 58\relax\mathord{\Ty}_{-})}\tau\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{-}B}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=TransAd+-,right=}\\
\frac{\vdash\Gamma\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma^{d^{\prime}}\vdash\Theta}{\Gamma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{d^{\prime}}(\Theta\mathchar 58\relax\mathord{\Ty}_{d})\vdash\mathord{\uparrow}\mathrel{:}\Gamma}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=WkTy,right=($d,d^{\prime}:\Dir$)}\frac{\vdash\Gamma\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma^{d}\vdash\Theta}{\Gamma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{d}(\Theta\mathchar 58\relax\mathord{\Ty}_{+})\mathbin{\triangleright}_{d}\mathord{\Theta}[\mathord{\uparrow}^{d}]\vdash\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{ty}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=VarZTy,right=($d\mathrel{:}\Dir$)}

Figure 6.
Type variables
Rules for type variables. Context can be extended by type variables
indexed by a telescope and with a direction, and substitutions (resp. transformations)
can be accordingly extended by a type family (resp. family of adapters).
We have weakening and variable operations, too.

Type variables

Using telescopes and instantiations, we can turn to
type variables (
Fig.

6
),
which combine contravariance (
Section

3.1.3
), and
dependency (
Section

3.1.4
).
The context
\Gamma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{d^{\prime}}(\Theta\mathchar 58\relax\mathord{\Ty}_{d})

represents the extension of
\Gamma

with a new type variable, dependent of the telescope
\Theta
defined over
\Gamma^{d^{\prime}}
,
and assumed to have direction
d
.
Rule
6Type variables
gives the corresponding substitution extension:
a type variable
{(\Theta\mathchar 58\relax\mathord{\Ty}_{d})}
must be replaced by a type with direction
d

in a context extended by
\Theta
.
Rules
6Type variables
and
6Type variables

give transformations’ extension for covariant telescopes. The biggest difference is
the variance of the adapter: in the latter
rule, the direction of the adapter is reversed compared to the former.
Similar rules applying to telescopes with a contravariant dependency over
the context are provided in
Section

D.6
.

It might not be evident why in these rules the direction of the adapter coincides
with that of the types substituted for the variable: we could imagine
contravariant adapters between covariant types or vice-versa.
However, this constraint arises from whiskering.
Indeed, consider the data in the diagram below, from which we construct

(\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}A)\circ\mu\mathrel{:}\Trans(\Gamma,\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}\mathord{A}[\sigma],\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}\mathord{A}[\tau])
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathchar 58\relax\mathord{\Ty}_{+})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}A}

The obvious computation rule is to simplify it to
\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}\mathord{A}\llbracket\mu\rrbracket
,
which is only valid if
A
is covariant.

Moreover, Rules
6Type variables
,

6Type variables
and
6Type variables

are in line with the action of dualization, as directions are just right for
the following equations to be type-correct:

\Gamma\vdash(\sigma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{d}A)^{-}\equiv\sigma^{-}\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{-d}A\mathrel{:}\Delta\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{d^{\prime}}(\Theta\mathchar 58\relax\mathord{\Ty}_{d})\Gamma\vdash(\mu\mathbin{\triangleright}_{d}f)^{-}\equiv\mu^{-}\mathbin{\triangleright}_{-d}f\mathrel{:}\tau\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{-d}B\Rightarrow\sigma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{-d}A

Finally, as for term variables, we have a weakening operation, and a variable zero

\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{ty}}
. This variable lives in an extended context, corresponding to the
extra binders it has access to. These would typically be instantiated
by providing an instantiation, and indeed we can derive

\frac{\vdash\Gamma\quad\Gamma^{d}\vdash\Theta\quad\Gamma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{d}(\Theta\mathchar 58\relax\mathord{\Ty}_{+})\vdash\Theta^{\prime}\quad\Gamma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{d}(\Theta\mathchar 58\relax\mathord{\Ty}_{+})\mathbin{\triangleright}\Theta^{\prime}\vdash\iota\mathrel{:}\Theta}{\Gamma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{d}(\Theta\mathchar 58\relax\mathord{\Ty}_{+})\mathbin{\triangleright}\Theta^{\prime}\vdash\mathord{\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{ty}}}[\mathord{\uparrow}_{\Theta^{\prime}}\mathbin{\triangleright}\iota]}\quad\textsc{right=($d:\Dir$)}

That is, if we access a type variable and provide an instantiation of its binders,
then we obtain a type in the current context,
i.e.
without extra extension.

3.3.
Back to AdapTT and natural models

A type former
\Gamma\vdash F
in AdapTT
2

is automatically equipped with a functorial structure with respect to its category of parameters represented by the context
\Gamma
.
This structure can be transferred to any NatMod
DO
,

i.e.
model of AdapTT, which interprets the underlying type former
F
.
To establish this correspondence, we first explain what it means for a NatMod
DO

C
to support a given type former in terms of presheaves over
C
, and how the existence of a structural type cast derives from an internal category structure on these presheaves.
Such an internal category in presheaves over
C
can equivalently be seen
as a
\Cat
-valued presheaf over
C
.
The main theorem of this section shows that, for any NatMod
DO

C
,
these
\Cat
-valued presheaves
\Pst(C)\coloneq C^{\op}\to\Cat

on
C
support a model of AdapTT
2
.
Applying this interpretation to
\Gamma\vdash F
in
\Pst(C)
thus
yields a structural type cast on the interpretation of the underlying type former in
C
.

Definition 3.1 (Type former in a natural model).

A type former in a natural model
C
is a pair
(D:\Psh(C),F:D\overset{\mathchar 58\relax}{\to}{}\Ty)
of a presheaf
D
and a natural transformation
F
to the presheaf of types.

For a context
\Gamma\mathrel{:}C
, the object
D\ \Gamma
represents the input parameters of the type former
(D,F)
, while
F
turn such data
d\mathrel{:}D\ \Gamma
into a type
F_{\Gamma}\ d\mathrel{:}\Ty(\Gamma)
.

Example 3.2 (
\P
type former).

In a natural model, the
\P
type is given
Awodey, 2018
, Prop. 2.4

by

\displaystyle D^{\P}\ \Gamma

\displaystyle\coloneq(A\mathrel{:}\Ty(\Gamma))\times(B\mathrel{:}\Ty(\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}A))

\displaystyle F^{\P}_{\Gamma}\ (A,B)\coloneq\P A\mathchar 58\relax B\mathrel{:}\Ty\ \Gamma

Definition 3.3 (Functorial structure on a type former).

A type former
(D,F:D\overset{\mathchar 58\relax}{\to}{}\Ty)
in the natural model underlying
a NatMod
DO

C
has a functorial structure when
D
is equipped with an internal category structure and
F
is an internal functor from
D
to
\Ty
,
equipped with its internal category structure of adapters.

Given a functorial structure on a type former
(D,F)
and a context
\Gamma\mathrel{:}C
, the morphisms between inputs
d,d^{\prime}\mathrel{:}D\ \Gamma
represents the data needed to build an adapter between
F_{\Gamma}\ d
and
F_{\Gamma}\ d^{\prime}
,
while the functorial action of
F_{\Gamma}
builds this adapter.
Going back to the example of
\P
-types, we could equip
(D^{\P},F^{\P})
with the functorial structure of
Example

2.1
.
But instead we can
derive
this structure from an interpretation, via

Theorem

3.4
, of the judgment

\Ty_{-}\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{ty}}\mathchar 58\relax\Ty_{+})\vdash\P\mathrel{:}\Ty
in AdapTT
2
, corresponding to an intrinsically functorial presentation of
\P
-types.

To properly establish the correspondence between NatMod
DO
s models of AdapTT and AdapTT
2
, we need conditions on the size of the models.
Given a universe
\mathcal{U}
, a category
C
is
\mathcal{U}
-small if its types of objects and homsets are in
\mathcal{U}
.
A NatMod
DO

C
is
\mathcal{U}
-small if its underlying category is and the

\Ty
and
\Tm
presheaves land into
\mathcal{U}
-small categories.

Theorem 3.4.

Given a
\mathcal{U}
-small NatMod
DO

C
, the 2-category
\Pst(C)
of
\Cat
-valued presheaf is a model of 2-dimensional AdapTT
2
.

Proof sketch.

The construction follows ideas from
2
-level type theory
Annenkov et al., 2023
where the outer layer is interpreted as presheaves over a model of the inner layer.
Here, the inner layer is an instance of AdapTT while the outer layer is one of AdapTT
2
.
We leverage an alternative description of
\Pst(C)
as the
2
-category
\Cat(\Psh(C))
of categories, functors and natural transformations internal to presheaves over
C

Jacobs, 2001
, Ch. 7
.
To manipulate these structures internally to a presheaf category, we use extensional
\symsf{MLTT}

8
8

8

Or intensional
\symsf{MLTT}
extended with the principles of uniqueness of identity proof and function extensionality
Hofmann, 1997a
;
Winterhalter et al., 2019
.
following
Hofmann (1997b, Ch. 4)
.
Working within
\Psh(C)
, we construct the 2-category
\mathcal{U}
-small categories equipping it with all the components to interpret AdapTT
2
, akin to
\AtNextCite
Licata and Harper (2011)
model of directed 2-dimensional type theory in
\Cat
.

Categories, functor and natural transformation can be defined in a standard fashion in extensional
\symsf{MLTT}
.
Relativizing these constructions to
\mathcal{U}
, seen as an internal universe using Hofmann-Streicher lifting
Awodey, 2024
, we use
\mathcal{U}\!\text{-}\!\Cat
, the
2
-category of
\mathcal{U}
-small categories, as the interpretation of
\Ctx
.

\mathcal{U}\!\text{-}\!\Cat
has a terminal object
\mathord{\diamondsuit}
, the category with a single object and morphism.
A type
A
in a context
\Gamma\mathrel{:}\mathcal{U}\!\text{-}\!\Cat
is interpreted as a functor
A\mathrel{:}\Gamma\to\mathcal{U}\!\text{-}\!\Cat
, substitutions acts by precomposition and transformations by
whiskering.
A term
t
of type
A
in
\Gamma
is interpreted as a lax natural transformation
\mathord{\diamondsuit}\overset{\mathchar 58\relax}{\to}{}A

Johnson and Yau, 2021
, definition 4.2.1
, where
\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathrel{:}\Gamma\to\mathcal{U}\!\text{-}\!\Cat
is the constant functor to
\mathord{\diamondsuit}
.
An adapter
f
between types
A
and
B
in
\Gamma
is interpreted as a
lax natural transformation
f\mathrel{:}A\overset{\mathchar 58\relax}{\to}{}B
, and acts on a term
t\mathrel{:}\mathord{\diamondsuit}\overset{\mathchar 58\relax}{\to}A
by vertical composition
f\circ t\mathrel{:}\mathord{\diamondsuit}_{\Gamma}\overset{\mathchar 58\relax}{\to}B
.
In these two cases, substitutions interpreted as functors act by whiskering and transformations interpreted as natural transformations by horizontal composition which preserve lax natural transformations.
All these operations are functorial on the nose since they are interpreted by composition in a strict
2
-category.

Context extensions are interpreted by variants on the Grothendieck construction:
positive context extension
\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}_{+}A
is interpreted by
\int_{\Gamma}A
while the negative context extension
\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}_{-}B
is interpreted by
\left(\int_{\Gamma^{\op}}B\right)^{\op}
.
The interpretation of telescopes and instantiations reuses that of types and terms.
The empty telescope on
\Gamma
is interpreted as
\mathord{\diamondsuit}_{\Gamma}\mathrel{:}\Gamma\to\mathcal{U}\!\text{-}\!\Cat
.
For a telescope
\Theta:\Gamma\to\mathcal{U}\!\text{-}\!\Cat
over
\Gamma
and a type
A\mathrel{:}\int_{\Gamma}\Theta\to\mathcal{U}\!\text{-}\!\Cat
, the extension
\Theta\mathbin{\triangleright}A
is interpreted as the functor
\gamma\mathrel{:}\Gamma\mapsto\int_{\Theta(\gamma)}A(\gamma,\cdot)
.
The final missing piece is the interpretation of type variables.
Observe that
\mathcal{U}\!\text{-}\!\Cat
is cartesian closed as a
1
-category and take
\Rightarrow:\mathcal{U}\!\text{-}\!\Cat^{\op}\times\mathcal{U}\!\text{-}\!\Cat\to\mathcal{U}\!\text{-}\!\Cat
the bifunctor sending categories
A,B
to their functor category.
The assumption that the
\Cat
-valued presheaf
\Ty
is externally
\mathcal{U}
-small implies that
\Ty\mathrel{:}\mathcal{U}\!\text{-}\!\Cat
, as well as its opposite category
\Ty^{\op}\mathrel{:}\mathcal{U}\!\text{-}\!\Cat
where the direction of adapters is flipped.
Interpreting direction
+
as the identity functor
\mathcal{U}\!\text{-}\!\Cat\to\mathcal{U}\!\text{-}\!\Cat
and
-
as the opposite category functor, for any context
\Gamma
and telescope
\Theta:\Gamma^{d}\to\mathcal{U}\!\text{-}\!\Cat
, the interpretation of
\Gamma\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{d}(\Theta\mathchar 58\relax\Ty_{d^{\prime}})
proceeds by first composing the pair of
\Theta^{\op}:\Gamma^{-d}\to\mathcal{U}\!\text{-}\!\Cat^{\op}
and the constant functor with value
\Ty^{d^{\prime}}
with the bifunctor
\Rightarrow
which yield
\Theta^{\op}{\Rightarrow}\Ty^{d^{\prime}}:\Gamma^{-d}\to\mathcal{U}\!\text{-}\!\Cat
, and then taking its Grothendieck construction
(\int_{\Gamma^{-d}}\Theta^{\op}{\Rightarrow}\Ty^{d^{\prime}})^{-d}
.
∎

Expressivity of AdapTT
2
’s type variables

AdapTT
2
does not offer many constructions on type variables: there are no
\Sigma
or
\Pi
formers that apply to type variables, even though the model sketched above would support such constructions.
This design is chosen to express the functoriality rules required for type formers in AdapTT, only tracking the functorial dependencies between families of parameters.
The restriction of telescopes in family variables to term variables is compatible with a simple model where every type is discrete and enough to express many type formers we are interested in.
Extending these quantifications to family variables, which stand for non-discrete categories, would extend the expressivity of the framework but also require more intricate tracking on the directed aspect, as can be found in
Licata and Harper, 2011
with restrictions on the combination of polarities.

4.
Functorial types

With the underlying judgmental structure sorted out, we can turn back to adding interesting
type formers to AdapTT
2
. Normally,
to specify a type former one gives rules for
type formation, introduction, elimination, computation (β) and, possibly, uniqueness (η)

The Univalent Foundations Program, 2013
, Rem. 1.5.1
.
We worked hard to make sure that the type formation rule amounts to giving a context, as showcased in
Section

3.1
.
This automatically implies a typing rule for the adapter —by Rule
6Type variables
—, as well as the functoriality equations.
Thus, to extend a usual type former to integrate adapters, the main remaining
task is to specify the interaction of the adapter with
introduction and elimination forms.

4.1.
Negative types: functions and pairs

\frac{\Delta\vdash\sigma,\tau\mathrel{:}\Gamma_{\P}\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta\vdash\mu\mathrel{:}\sigma\Rightarrow\tau}{\Delta\vdash\mathord{\P}\llbracket\mu\rrbracket\mathrel{:}\mathord{\P}[\sigma]\Rightarrow\mathord{\P}[\tau]}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=AdFun,right=}\frac{\Delta\vdash\sigma,\tau\mathrel{:}\Gamma_{\Sig}\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta\vdash\mu\mathrel{:}\sigma\Rightarrow\tau}{\Delta\vdash\mathord{\Sig}\llbracket\mu\rrbracket\mathrel{:}\mathord{\Sig}[\sigma]\Rightarrow\mathord{\Sig}[\tau]}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=AdPair,right=}\frac{\Delta\vdash a\mathrel{:}A^{\prime}\Rightarrow A\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta\mathbin{\triangleright}A^{\prime}\vdash b\mathrel{:}\mathord{B}[\symup{id}\mathbin{\mathord{\triangleright}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\triangleright}}a]\Rightarrow B^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta\vdash f\mathrel{:}\mathord{\P}[A\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}B]\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta\vdash u\mathrel{:}A^{\prime}}{\Delta\vdash f\langle\mathord{\P}\llbracket a\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}b\rrbracket\rangle~u\equiv(f~u\langle a\rangle)\langle\mathord{b}[\symup{id}\mathbin{\triangleright}u\langle a\rangle]\rangle\mathrel{:}\mathord{B^{\prime}}[\symup{id}\mathbin{\triangleright}u]}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=AdFunEq,right=}\frac{\Delta\vdash a\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow A^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta\mathbin{\triangleright}A\vdash b\mathrel{:}B\Rightarrow\mathord{B^{\prime}}[\symup{id}\mathbin{\mathord{\triangleright}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\triangleright}}a]\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta\vdash p\mathrel{:}\mathord{\Sig}[A\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}B]}{\Delta\vdash\pi_{1}~(p\langle\mathord{\Sig}\llbracket a\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}b\rrbracket\rangle)\equiv(\pi_{1}~p)\langle a\rangle\mathrel{:}A^{\prime}\\

and

types
Typing and computation rules for adapters of dependent function
and pair types. The first are derived from the given context, computation of
a function adapter amounts to pre/post composition, and computation for a
pair adapter applies the relevant adapter to each projection.

and

), we can essentially follow

.

.
If we compute concretely what a substitution/transformation targeting

\W
and
\P

and
3.1.4.
\Sig
: dependency
from

3.1.4
.
We can derive a contravariant version of the rules for free
by instantiating them in a dualized context, for instance
\P A\mathchar 58\relax B
is

B
is and
A

Adapters compute according to the types’ eliminator, following the idea
that negative types are characterized by their eliminators.
Extending AdapTT
2
with
\l
-abstraction and application,
we recover equations on constructors,
e.g.

(\l t)\langle\mathord{\P}\llbracket a\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}b\rrbracket\rangle\equiv\l(\mathord{t}[\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{tm}}\langle a\rangle])\langle b\rangle

via η expansion.

Moreover, the functoriality laws up to casts are derivable from the computation
equations only. Indeed, we can obtain

f\langle\mathord{\P}\llbracket(a_{2}\circ a_{1})\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}(b_{2}\circ b_{1})\rrbracket\rangle\equiv f\langle\mathord{\P}\llbracket a_{1}\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}b_{1}\rrbracket\circ\mathord{\P}\llbracket a_{2}\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}b_{2}\rrbracket\rangle
,
by first η-expanding both sides, then triggering
7Adapters for

⁢
\symup
Π

and

⁢
\symup

to
compute the
\P
adapters away, and conclude by functoriality of coercion,

i.e.
the fact that
t\langle a^{\prime}\circ a\rangle\equiv t\langle a\rangle\langle a^{\prime}\rangle
.
The situation for
\mathord{\P}\llbracket\symup{id}\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}\symup{id}\rrbracket
and
\Sig
is similar.

4.2.
Inductive types

To represent inductive types, we cannot rely on encodings through
e.g.

\W
types, as
these necessarily rely on term-level manipulations —for instance
A+B
is encoded
as
\Sig(b:\symsf{Bool})\mathchar 58\relax\operatorname{if}b\operatorname{then}A\operatorname{else}B
,
by using the large eliminator for booleans.
As we do not internalize adapters as a type, such an encoding would yield a degenerate notion of
adapters for the
+
type, which is unsatisfactory.

Instead, we provide a general scheme for parametrized and indexed inductive types,
capturing a class corresponding to that
originally described by
Coquand and Paulin (1990)
;
Paulin-Mohring (1993)
,
and still used as the backbone of most proof assistants.
We describe this class using a theory of signatures, inspired by
multiple existing ones
Kovács, 2022
;
Kaposi and von Raumer, 2020
;
Escot and Cockx, 2022
.
Compared to the former two
Kovács, 2022
;
Kaposi and von Raumer, 2020
, we focus more closely
on parameters, as it is with respect to them
that (inductive) type formers are functorial, and use the machinery
of type variables and telescopes from

Section

3.2
in the signatures, rather than
relying on an external product type and universe.
Our descriptions are closer to the latter
Escot and Cockx, 2022
,
although we frame them using GATs rather than of a universe of
description.

Extended GAT

To lighten the rules, we rely on two extensions
to the standard setting of generalized algebraic theories.
The first is the list type —at the meta-level of the GAT—,
which we denote with a vector notation. We still use
\mathord{\diamondsuit}
and
\mathbin{\triangleright}
for
nil and cons,
[x;y;\dots]
for an explicit list, and
c\in cs

for witnesses that
c
appears in a list
cs
, which are essentially de Bruijn indices.
Second, we allow ourselves a form of record types, expressed as follows:

\frac{\vdash\Gamma_{par}\quad\Gamma_{par}\vdash\Theta_{ind}\quad\Gamma_{par}\vdash\Theta_{nr}\quad r\mathrel{:}\overrightarrow{\RecDesc(\Gamma_{par},\Theta_{ind},\Theta_{nr})}\quad\Gamma_{par}\mathbin{\triangleright}\Theta_{nr}\vdash\iota\mathrel{:}\mathord{\Theta_{ind}}[\mathord{\uparrow}]}{\{\symsf{nrec}\coloneq\Theta_{nr};\symsf{rec}\coloneq r;\symsf{ind}\coloneq\iota\}\mathrel{:}\ConDesc(\Gamma_{par},\Theta_{ind})}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}

This rule adds a new “record sort”
\ConDesc
, indexed by a context and a telescope,
with three fields
\symsf{nrec}
,
\symsf{rec}
and
\symsf{ind}
,
accessed by
c\mathchar 58\relax_{\symsf{\symsf{nrec}}}
.
These extensions can be encoded in standard GATs at the cost of introducing
new sorts, operations and equations, which would only add distraction.

\frac{\vdash\Gamma_{par}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma_{par}\vdash\Theta_{ind}}{\IndDesc(\Gamma_{par},\Theta_{ind})\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\overrightarrow{\ConDesc(\Gamma_{par},\Theta_{ind})}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=DataDesc,right=}\frac{\vdash\Gamma_{par}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma_{par}\vdash\Theta_{ind}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma_{par}\vdash\Theta_{nr}\hskip 9.24994ptr\mathrel{:}\overrightarrow{\RecDesc(\Gamma_{par},\Theta_{ind},\Theta_{nr})}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma_{par}\mathbin{\triangleright}\Theta_{nr}\vdash\iota\mathrel{:}\mathord{\Theta_{ind}}[\mathord{\uparrow}]}{\{\symsf{nrec}\coloneq\Theta_{nr};\symsf{rec}\coloneq r;\symsf{ind}\coloneq\iota\}\mathrel{:}\ConDesc(\Gamma_{par},\Theta_{ind})}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=ConDesc,right=}\\
\frac{\vdash\Gamma_{par}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma_{par}\vdash\Theta_{ind}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma_{par}\vdash\Theta_{nr}\hskip 9.24994pt(\Gamma_{par}\mathbin{\triangleright}\Theta_{nr})^{-}\vdash\Theta_{ar}\hskip 9.24994pt(\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}_{+}\Theta_{nr})\mathbin{\triangleright}_{-}\Theta_{ar}\vdash\iota\mathrel{:}\mathord{\Theta_{ind}}[\mathord{\uparrow}]}{\{\symsf{arit}\coloneq\Theta_{ar};\symsf{rind}\coloneq\iota\}\mathrel{:}\RecDesc(\Gamma_{par},\Theta_{ind},\Theta_{nr})}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=RecDesc,right=}

Figure 8.
Signatures for inductive types, constructors and recursive arguments
Formation rules for a well-formed inductive signature, relying
on that for a well-formed constructor, itself relying on that for a well-formed
recursive position description.

Signatures

Signatures, presented in
Fig.

8
, are built out of three additional sorts: inductive descriptions
\IndDesc
, constructors descriptions
\ConDesc
and recursive argument descriptions
\RecDesc
.
The description of an inductive type
I\mathrel{:}\IndDesc
is indexed by
a context
\Gamma_{par}
of parameters and
a telescope
\Theta_{ind}
of indices, and consists of a list
of constructor descriptions.
A constructor description
c\mathrel{:}\ConDesc

consists of a telescope
\symsf{nrec}
of non-recursive arguments,
a list
\symsf{rec}
of recursive argument description, and a final instantiation

\symsf{ind}
of indices, which can depend on the non-recursive arguments
9
9

9

Allowing indices to depend on recursive arguments would yield descriptors for inductive-inductive type former.
This is also what allows separating non-recursive arguments and recursive ones without losing generality.
.
Finally, the description of a recursive argument
r\mathrel{:}\RecDesc
is given by a telescope of arities
\symsf{arit}
binding additional parameters for a recursive argument,
corresponding to a “branching” argument, and an instantiation
\symsf{rind}

of the indices for the recursive occurrence.
Note that, crucially, the arity is a
contravariant
telescope,
as an adapter acts on recursive arguments’ parameters by pre-composition,
generalizing the examples of trees in
Sections

3.1.3
and
3.1.4
.

Example 4.1 (List).

\List
has one covariant type parameter and no indices,
i.e.

\Gamma_{\List}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{+}(\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathchar 58\relax\mathord{\Ty}_{+})
is the context of
parameters and
\mathord{\diamondsuit}
the telescope of indices.
The description is given by

\displaystyle c_{nil}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\{\symsf{nrec}\coloneq\mathord{\diamondsuit};\symsf{rec}\coloneq\mathord{\diamondsuit};\symsf{ind}\coloneq\mathord{\diamondsuit}\}\mathrel{:}\ConDesc(\Gamma_{\List},\mathord{\diamondsuit})

\displaystyle c_{cons}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\{\symsf{nrec}\coloneq\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathbin{\triangleright}\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{ty}};\symsf{rec}\coloneq[\{\symsf{arit}\coloneq\mathord{\diamondsuit};\symsf{rind}\coloneq\mathord{\diamondsuit}\}];\symsf{ind}\coloneq\mathord{\diamondsuit}\}\mathrel{:}\ConDesc(\Gamma_{\List},\mathord{\diamondsuit})

\displaystyle\mathord{\operatorname{listDesc}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}[c_{nil};c_{cons}]\mathrel{:}\IndDesc(\Gamma_{\List},\mathord{\diamondsuit})

The constructor nil has no arguments, and cons has two arguments,
a non-recursive one typed by the unique type parameter, and a recursive one with
empty arity,
i.e.
no branching.

We omit the type
\symsf{Nat}
, which is similar.
We use
\mathord{\operatorname{0}}
and
\mathord{\operatorname{S}}
for its two constructors.

The type of vectors is similar to lists, except that there is now an index of type

\symsf{Nat}
,
i.e.
we take
\Theta_{\mathord{\operatorname{Vec}}}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathbin{\triangleright}\symsf{Nat}\mathrel{:}\Tel(\Gamma_{\List})
. Constructors are given by

\displaystyle c_{nil}

\displaystyle c_{cons}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\{\symsf{nrec}\coloneq\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathbin{\triangleright}\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{ty}}\mathbin{\triangleright}\symsf{Nat};\symsf{rec}\coloneq[\{\symsf{arit}\coloneq\mathord{\diamondsuit};\symsf{rind}\coloneq\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathbin{\triangleright}\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{tm}}\}];\symsf{ind}\coloneq\mathord{\mathord{\operatorname{S}}}[\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{tm}}]\}

\displaystyle\qquad\mathrel{:}\ConDesc(\Gamma_{\List},\Theta_{\mathord{\operatorname{Vec}}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}[c_{nil};c_{cons}]\mathrel{:}\IndDesc(\Gamma_{\List},\Theta_{\mathord{\operatorname{Vec}}})

The index is instantiated in both constructors,

Example 4.3 (Sum).

The sum type has two non-dependent, covariant parameters, thus we take

\Gamma_{+}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{+}(\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathchar 58\relax\mathord{\Ty}_{+})\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{+}(\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathchar 58\relax\mathord{\Ty}_{+})

\displaystyle c_{inl}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\{\symsf{nrec}\coloneq\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathbin{\triangleright}\mathord{\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{ty}}}[\mathord{\uparrow}];\symsf{rec}\coloneq\mathord{\diamondsuit};\symsf{ind}\coloneq\mathord{\diamondsuit}\}\mathrel{:}\ConDesc(\Gamma_{+},\mathord{\diamondsuit})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\{\symsf{nrec}\coloneq\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathbin{\triangleright}\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{ty}};\symsf{rec}\coloneq\mathord{\diamondsuit};\symsf{ind}\coloneq\mathord{\diamondsuit}\}\mathrel{:}\ConDesc(\Gamma_{+},\mathord{\diamondsuit})

We have two constructors with one non-recursive argument, of either

Example 4.4 (Trees).

The type

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\W}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{+}(\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathchar 58\relax\mathord{\Ty}_{+})\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{+}(\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathbin{\triangleright}\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{ty}}\mathchar 58\relax\mathord{\Ty}_{-})\mathrel{:}\Ctx

Example 4.5 (Identity).

The identity type has two parameters, a type and a term of that type,
and one index, again a term of that type.
10
10

10

This is the “based” identity type. Alternatively, we could consider
both terms to be indices.

The single
constructor for reflexivity has no arguments, and its index is the second
parameter, thus

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\Id}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{+}(\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathchar 58\relax\mathord{\Ty}_{+})\mathbin{\triangleright}\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{ty}}\hskip 50.00008pt\Theta_{\Id}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathbin{\triangleright}\mathord{\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{ty}}}[\mathord{\uparrow}]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\{\symsf{nrec}\coloneq\mathord{\diamondsuit};\symsf{rec}\coloneq\mathord{\diamondsuit};\symsf{ind}\coloneq\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{tm}}\}\mathrel{:}\ConDesc(\Gamma_{\Id},\Theta_{\Id})

\displaystyle\mathord{\operatorname{idDesc}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}[c_{refl}]\mathrel{:}\IndDesc(\Gamma_{\Id},\Theta_{\Id})

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I\mathrel{:}\IndDesc(\Gamma_{par},\Theta_{ind})}\hskip 9.24994pt}{\Gamma_{par}\mathbin{\triangleright}\Theta_{ind}\vdash\ind(I)}\hskip 58\relax_{\symsf{ind}}}[\mathord{\uparrow}]]}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=IndCstr,right=}\frac{c\mathrel{:}\ConDesc(\Gamma_{par},\Theta_{ind})\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=ConDataDef,right=}\frac{\vdash\Gamma_{par}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma_{par}\vdash\Theta_{ind}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma_{par}\vdash\Theta_{nr}\hskip \hskip 92.49939pt\P\mathord{r\mathchar 58\relax_{\symsf{arit}}}[\mathord{\uparrow}\mathbin{\mathord{\triangleright}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\triangleright}}\symup{id}]\mathchar 58\relax\left(\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{ty}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[(\mathord{\uparrow}\circ\mathord{\uparrow})\mathbin{\triangleright}(\mathord{r\mathchar 58\relax_{\symsf{rind}}}[(\mathord{\uparrow}\circ\mathord{\uparrow})\mathbin{\triangleright}\mathord{\vinst}[\mathord{\uparrow}]\mathbin{\triangleright}\vinst]\right)])}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=RecDataDef,right=}}

Figure 9.
Inductive type and term constructors
Typing rules for inductive operations: inductive type, inductive
constructor (relying on constructor data and recursive data).

Constructors

Given a signature, we first construct the corresponding type and
term constructors, in
Fig.

9
.
The inductive type,
9Inductive type and term constructors
, is easy:
we simply read off the parameters and indices from the description,
and declare the well-formedness of a type in the former extended by the latter.
As explained in
Section

3.1.1
, this suffices to recover the type
in an arbitrary context by substitution.

For constructors, Rule
9Inductive type and term constructors
is a bit more complicated, and
relies on an auxiliary telescope
\conData
, whose instantiations correspond
to the arguments of the constructor.
This telescope is computed with respect to an extra variable depending on
the telescope of indices, and the loop is tied
by instantiating this variable with the inductive itself.
In turn,
\conData
is computed by concatenating the telescope of non-recursive
arguments and that of recursive arguments. The latter are computed from their description by

\recData
, which, for each recursive argument, builds a
\P
with domain
the arity and codomain the extra type variable, properly instantiated.

Adapters

\frac{I\mathrel{:}\IndDesc(\Gamma_{par},\mathord{\diamondsuit})\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c\mathrel{:}\ConDesc({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{par},\mathord{\diamondsuit}})}\hskip 9.24994ptic\mathrel{:}c\in I\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta\vdash\mu\mathrel{:}p\Rightarrow_{\Gamma_{par}}p^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta\vdash arg\mathrel{:}\mathord{\conData}[p\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}\mathord{\ind(I)}[p]]}{\Delta\vdash\mathord{\constr(ic)}[p\mathbin{\triangleright}arg]\langle\mathord{\ind(I)}\llbracket\mu\rrbracket\rangle\equiv\hskip 106.3743pt\\
\hskip 27.74982pt\mathord{\constr(ic)}[p^{\prime}\mathbin{\triangleright}arg\langle\mathord{\left(\mathord{\conData(c)}[\symup{id}\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}\ind(I)]\right)}\llbracket\mu\rrbracket\rangle]\mathrel{:}\mathord{\ind(I)}[p^{\prime}]}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=IndAdEqPar,right=}\frac{I\mathrel{:}\IndDesc(\Gamma_{par},\Theta_{ind})\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c\mathrel{:}\ConDesc({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{par},\Theta_{ind}})}\hskip 9.24994ptic\mathrel{:}c\in I\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta\vdash\mu\mathrel{:}p\Rightarrow_{\Gamma_{par}}p^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta\vdash argn\mathrel{:}\mathord{c\mathchar 58\relax_{\symsf{nrec}}}[p]\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta\vdash argr\mathrel{:}\mathord{\recDatas(c\mathchar 58\relax_{\symsf{rec}})}[p\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}\mathord{\ind(I)}[p\mathbin{\mathord{\triangleright}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\triangleright}}\symup{id}]\mathbin{\triangleright}argn]}{\Delta\vdash\mathord{\constr(ic)}[p\mathbin{\triangleright}argn\mathbin{\triangleright}argr]\langle\mathord{\ind(I)}\llbracket\mu\mathbin{\mathord{\triangleright}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\triangleright}}\mathord{c\mathchar 58\relax_{\symsf{ind}}}[p\mathbin{\triangleright}argn]\rrbracket\rangle\equiv\hskip 27.74982pt\\
\mathord{\constr(ic)}[p^{\prime}\mathbin{\triangleright}(argn\mathbin{\triangleright}argr)\langle\mathord{\left(\mathord{\conData(c)}[\symup{id}\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}\ind(I)]\right)}\llbracket\mu\rrbracket\rangle]\\
\hskip 64.74957pt\mathrel{:}\mathord{\ind(I)}[p^{\prime}\mathbin{\triangleright}\mathord{c\mathchar 58\relax_{\symsf{ind}}}[p\mathbin{\triangleright}argn]\langle{\mathord{\Theta_{ind}}\llbracket\mu\rrbracket}\rangle]}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=IndAdEq,right=}

Figure 10.
Inductive adapter equation, without and with indices
Computation rule for inductive adapters on a constructor. A very
generalised version of a map function, relying on the action of a transformation
on a telescope.

From the data of
Fig.

9
, we can already derive
an adapter
\mathord{\ind(I)}\llbracket\tilde{\mu}\rrbracket
given any

\Delta\vdash\tilde{\mu}\mathrel{:}pi\Rightarrow_{\Gamma_{par}\mathbin{\triangleright}\Theta_{ind}}pi^{\prime}
.
The missing piece is to give equality rules for
the action of this adapter on
\constr
.
First, by extensionality for substitutions, such a
\tilde{\mu}

must be of the form

\Delta\vdash\mu\mathbin{\triangleright}\iota\mathrel{:}p\mathbin{\triangleright}\iota\Rightarrow_{\Gamma_{par}\mathbin{\triangleright}\Theta_{ind}}p^{\prime}\mathbin{\triangleright}\iota\langle\mathord{\Theta_{ind}}\llbracket\mu\rrbracket\rangle

for
\Delta\vdash\mu\mathrel{:}p\Rightarrow_{\Gamma_{par}}p^{\prime}

and
\Delta\vdash\iota\mathrel{:}\mathord{\Theta_{ind}}[p]
. That is,
any “inductive adapter” consists of a transformation
between two parameter substitutions, and a constraint between
indices of the source and target types. Following this decomposition, we aim
to characterize a term of the form

\mathord{\constr(ic)}[p\mathbin{\triangleright}argn\mathbin{\triangleright}argr]\langle\mathord{\ind(I)}\llbracket\mu\mathbin{\mathord{\triangleright}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\triangleright}}\mathord{c\mathchar 58\relax_{\symsf{ind}}}[p\mathbin{\triangleright}argn]\rrbracket\rangle

Indeed, as the term constructor is given in its universal context in

Fig.

9
, we must also account for a substitution

p\mathbin{\triangleright}argn\mathbin{\triangleright}argr
.

In the end, we obtain Rule
10Inductive adapter equation, without and with indices

of
Fig.

10
. The core idea is more readable
without indices, in the simplified Rule
10Inductive adapter equation, without and with indices
:
we rely on the fact that
\conData
is a telescope to derive
an action of the transformation
\mu
on it, which provides an
adapter by which we can adapt the arguments of the constructor.
The adapter we use,

\mathord{\left(\mathord{\conData(c)}[\symup{id}\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}\ind(I)]\right)}\llbracket\mu\rrbracket
,
is obtained directly from the type of
arg
. However, we can compute it
with the equation for the whiskering, and derive it is equal to

\mathord{\conData(c)}\llbracket\left(\mu\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}\mathord{\ind(I)}\llbracket\mu\rrbracket\right)\rrbracket
.
This intuitively corresponds to the fact that the transformation acts on the

\conData
by using
\mu
for the parameters, and recursively relies on

\mathord{\ind(I)}\llbracket\mu\rrbracket
for recursive positions.
The final rule is more noisy, as it has to account for indices, but as
these are essentially constrained by a definitional equality, there is not much
more happening on their end.

We can now revisit
Examples

4.1
,
4.2

4.3

4.4
and
4.5
in

.

Non-Example 4.7 (Mixed variance).

Types in AdapTT
2
being fully covariant (or contravariant)
slightly limits our expressivity.
Consider the (simplified version of the) type of indexed
trees,
\IW
, given by parameters

\Gamma_{\IW}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}(I:\Ty_{+})\,(X:\Ty_{+})\,(Y:\Ty_{-})\,(d:X\to Y\to I)
,
indices
\Theta_{\IW}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}I
, and constructor

\mathord{\operatorname{sup}}\mathrel{:}\P(i:I)\,(x:X)\,(\P(y:Y)\mathchar 58\relax\IW I\,X\,Y\,(d\,x\,y))\mathchar 58\relax\IW X\,Y\,i
.
However, as
Y\to I\mathrel{:}\Ty_{+}
but
X\mathrel{:}\Ty_{+}
,
the type
X\to Y\to I
of
d
is actually ill-formed. Making

X
contravariant instead is not a solution, as it would break the quantification
over
x:X
—and be inconsistent with plain
\W
.

This is not an oversight. Indeed,
if one tries to describe an ad-hoc adapter between
\IW\,I\,X\,Y\,d

and
\IW\,I^{\prime}\,X^{\prime}\,Y^{\prime}\,d^{\prime}
, one certainly needs
adapters
a_{I}\mathrel{:}\Ad(I,I^{\prime})
,
a_{A}\mathrel{:}\Ad(A,A^{\prime})
and
a_{B}\mathrel{:}\Ad(B^{\prime},B)
.
But when trying to write the constraint between
d
and
d^{\prime}
, one ends up needing

(x:X)\mathbin{\triangleright}(y:Y)\vdash\left(d\,x\langle a_{A}\rangle\,y\right)\langle a_{I}\rangle\equiv d^{\prime}\,x\,y\langle a_{B}\rangle\mathrel{:}I^{\prime}

The adapters
a_{I}
,
a_{A}
and
a_{B}

act
simultaneously on both sides
, which goes beyond
Rules
3.1.2.
Identity: transformations and terms
and
3.1.2.
Identity: transformations and terms
.
Describing such mixed actions seems possible using spans,
but this would be considerably more involved than our
current setup, so for now we refrain from exploring it.
This issue also explain why our telescopes have a uniform variance.

Recursors and fusion law

As it is mainly orthogonal to our
functoriality concerns and very tedious, we do not describe the derivation of
a recursor for our datatype descriptions here.

There is, however, one interaction worth investigating, that was dubbed
fusion law

Laurent et al., 2024
. In
Section

4.1
, we described the action of
adapters only on destructors, but, thanks to η laws, this
entailed a derivable equality for adapters on constructors. For
inductive types, we only have an equation for adapters on constructors,
which entails no equation for the recursor in the absence of an η law.
The fusion law is such an equation, saying that applying a recursor on a
term
t\langle\indAd(I,\mu)\rangle
amounts to applying to
t
a
recursor where
\mu
is pushed in the branches.
Categorically, this says that the family (over
\Gamma_{par}
)
of initial algebras
\ind(I)
is natural, in that
computing the section of a displayed algebra after the action of
\mu
coincides with
the section of a different displayed algebra, directly pre-composed with
\mu
.

Functorial type formers from descriptions

To wrap up this section, we revisit
Theorem

3.4

with inductive type descriptions at hand.
Unfolding the construction of the model of AdapTT over a NatMod
DO
, the constant introduced by a description
I\mathrel{:}\IndDesc(\Gamma_{par},\Theta_{ind})
in AdapTT
2

lets us compute the rules
C
must satisfy to support
the functorial inductive type former presented by
I
.

To illustrate this, consider the description of

\W
-types from
Example

4.4
.
The interpretation of
\Gamma_{\W}\coloneq(X:\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathchar 58\relax\mathord{\Ty}_{+})\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}_{+}(Y\mathrel{:}\mathord{\diamondsuit}\mathbin{\triangleright}\mathtt{0}^{\symsf{ty}}\mathchar 58\relax\mathord{\Ty}_{-})
via
Theorem

3.4

yields a
\Cat
-valued presheaf that can be evaluated at an object
\Gamma
of
C
: its objects are pairs
(\Gamma\vdash A,\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}(x:A)\vdash B)

of a type in
\Gamma
and a family over it,
while its morphisms are pairs of adapters
(\Gamma\vdash f\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow A^{\prime},\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}(x:A)\vdash g\mathrel{:}\mathord{B^{\prime}}[\symup{id}\mathbin{\triangleright}x\langle f\rangle]\Rightarrow B)
.
Requiring functorial
\W
types to exist then corresponds to the admissibility of the two following rules, whose premises have been computed from
\Gamma_{\W}
.

\small\frac{\Gamma\vdash A\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma,x:A\vdash B}{\W A\,B}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}\frac{\Gamma\vdash f\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow A^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}(x:A)\vdash g\mathrel{:}\mathord{B^{\prime}}[\symup{id}\mathbin{\triangleright}x\langle f\rangle]\Rightarrow B}{\Gamma\vdash\mathord{\W}\llbracket f\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}g\rrbracket\mathrel{:}\W\,A\,B\Rightarrow\W\,A^{\prime}\,B^{\prime}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}

We can also compute the action of this adapter on the constructor.

\frac{\Delta\vdash f\mathrel{:}A\Rightarrow A^{\prime}\quad\Delta\mathbin{\triangleright}(x:A)\vdash g\mathrel{:}\mathord{B^{\prime}}[\symup{id}\mathbin{\triangleright}x\langle f\rangle]\Rightarrow B\quad\Delta\vdash a\mathrel{:}A\quad\Gamma\mathbin{\triangleright}(b:\mathord{B}[\symup{id}\mathbin{\triangleright}a])\vdash k\mathrel{:}\W\,A\,B}{\Delta\vdash\mathord{\operatorname{sup}}\,a\,(\l b\mathchar 58\relax k)\langle\mathord{\W}\llbracket f\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}g\rrbracket\rangle\equiv\mathord{\operatorname{sup}}\,(a\langle f\rangle)\,(\l\,b^{\prime}\mathchar 58\relax(k\,(b^{\prime}\langle g\rangle))\langle\mathord{\W}\llbracket f\mathbin{\blacktriangleright}g\rrbracket\rangle)\mathrel{:}\W\,A^{\prime}\,B^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{vcenter,left=,right=}

In general, considering descriptions in AdapTT
2
which are more constrained
and informative thanks to variance information, we can derive
everything needed for a functorial type former
in the sense of
Definitions

3.1
and
3.3
:
the source category of the type former as functor, the way to construct
a type and adapter from this information, and the equations satisfied by
said adapter.

5.
Related work

Type casting

Type casting is ubiquitous in dependent type theory, and has been
studied in many forms. The most pervasive one is provided by propositional
equality, which itself comes in many flavours: inductive
Martin-Löf and Sambin, 1984
,
univalent
The Univalent Foundations Program, 2013
, cubical
Cohen et al., 2015
,
observational
Altenkirch et al., 2007
;
Pujet and Tabareau, 2022
… Observational equality is the
most directly structural, and it is hence the most natural to relate to AdapTT. However,
since we do not force adapters to be unique,
other forms of equality could a priori work with AdapTT,
similarly to
Example

2.9
. It might be particularly interesting
to explore how AdapTT could interact with the ongoing work of higher observational
type theory
Altenkirch et al., 2024
.

Another common approach to type casting is subtyping. Here the main
divide is between subsumptive subtyping, where the same underlying term is given
multiple types, and coercive subtyping, with an explicit operation.
11
11

11

The subsumptive/coercive terminology is due to
Luo (1999)
.

Subsumptive subtyping is closer to what users expect —not having to
insert coercions— but has a non-algebraic character which makes it
impossible to capture with GATs, and causes theoretical issues
Lungu and Luo, 2018
,
although it can be described with a more refined fibrational view

Melliès and Zeilberger, 2015
. However,
Laurent et al. (2024)
show that focusing
on coercive subtyping, which is the one AdapTT allows (see
Example

2.8
),
is not really an issue, as a flexible enough coercive system can emulate
a subsumptive one. For this, though, functoriality equations are paramount.

Comprehension categories

Coraglia and Emmenegger (2024a)
;
Coraglia and Emmenegger (2024b)
analyze comprehension categories
Jacobs, 1993
as models of type theory in terms of natural models and remark they carry structure akin to (proof-relevant) subtyping.

Najmaei et al. (2026)
further this comparison, chiefly motivated by semantics, and explore models generated by weak algebraic factorization system with a natural induced notion of morphism between types.
We arrive at a similar understanding from the opposite perspective: even though our initial motivation lie in the syntax for casts/subtyping, we also converge to the notion of comprehension categories.
This coincidence of two different research lines with rather different motivations is remarkable, and shows how natural NatMod
DO
s are.

The emphasis on models in these works comes with an important difference regarding
strictness:
Najmaei et al. (2026)
focus on a syntax faithfully representing comprehension categories, where fibrations are not necessarily split.
Hence, many correspondences are only up to isomorphism,
e.g.

\mathord{A}[\symup{id}]
and
A
are isomorphic rather than convertible.
As we aim primarily for a usable syntax, we were happy to strictify these equations –
as done in
Najmaei et al.
’s CCTT
split
–,
trading splitness for a lighter syntax.

Coraglia and Emmenegger
similarly mention
“[they] have written the action of reindexing as if the fibrations involved were split [which] allows [them] to simplify notation in the rules”
.

The treatment of functorial types presents a second difference.

Najmaei et al.
focus on a handful of types (
\P
,
\Sig
and
\Id
), for which they give dedicated rules in their equivalent CCTT of our AdapTT.
This presentation does not provide a general framework to capture the functoriality of types, as granted by AdapTT
2
.
On the other hand, they are careful to separate the structure for a given type former on a model from the extra subtyping structure for that type former.
We take the different view, embodied by AdapTT
2
, that
every
type (former) should be functorial — possibly with respect to a discrete source category.

Representing inductive types

Chapman et al. (2010)
pioneered the description of inductive types through a type of descriptions.

Escot and Cockx (2022)
leveraged similar descriptions for metaprogramming in
Agda
, introducing telescopes as we do.
Our notion of inductive descriptions remain external as a judgment, heavily inspired by the theories of signatures of
Kovács (2022)
;
Kaposi and von Raumer (2020)
.
The very structured descriptions provided by these approaches are crucial for us,
and we could not use encodings via (indexed) containers
Abbott et al., 2005
;
Altenkirch et al., 2015
,
which fundamentally rely on a detour via universes and large elimination.

Functoriality and parametricity

Beyond the works already
mentioned featuring structural coercions

Laurent et al., 2024
;
Lennon-Bertrand et al., 2022
;
Altenkirch et al., 2007
;
Pujet and Tabareau, 2022
;
Pujet et al., 2025
,
another line with strong connections to ours is that on univalent parametricity

Tabareau et al., 2018
. Although relying on (binary) parametricity rather than
functoriality, the end result is a form of transport that follows the structure
of type formers. The subsequent work on
Trocq

Cohen et al., 2024

extends this approach to a rich hierarchy of
possible relations between types along which one can (partially) transport,
from the basic existence of a function to full-blown equivalence,
the only one covered by univalent parametricity.
This careful study could inform the extension of AdapTT
2
with subtler forms of variance, while our work could inform how
to extend
Trocq
to handle inductive types.

Arkor (2022)
also models type variables in System F using 2-categories.

Directed and Multimodal Type Theories

Theorem

3.4
is directly inspired by the work of
Licata and Harper (2011)
on a
2
-dimensional directed type theory corresponding to
\Cat
.
Further work on
2
-dimensional directed type theory have focused on the internalization of morphisms as a type
North, 2019
;
Neumann and Altenkirch, 2024
and on bicategorical models
Ahrens et al., 2023
.

Garner (2009)
presents a
2
-dimensional variant of MLTT with
undirected

2
-cells as a step toward higher-models of identity types, recently extended to all type formers of
\symsf{MLTT}
with weak computational rules by
Spadetto (2025)
.
In the setting of homotopy type theory,
Riehl and Shulman (2017)
propose a framework for synthetic
(\infty,1)
-categories where every function between types behaving as categories (Segal types) are automatically functorial, with a cubical and constructive variant investigated by
Weaver and Licata (2020)
.

Gratzer et al. (2025)
design another variant based on simplicial type theory relying on multi-modal dependent type theory
Gratzer et al., 2021
which we could take inspiration from for extending AdapTT
2
.

Presheaf Models of Dependent Type Theory

Using
\AtNextCite
Hofmann (1997b, Ch. 4)
model of MLTT in presheaves is a recurrent theme in our work.

Uemura (2021)
proposes to use presheaf categories as a semantics for Second-Order Generalized Algebraic Theories (SOGAT), that we employ (
Fig.

12
) to give a concise description of AdapTT.
The articulation of AdapTT and AdapTT
2
is reminiscent of
2
-level type theory
Annenkov et al., 2023
and the internal construction of the
2
-category of categories mirrors the axiomatic construction of cubical models in
Orton and Pitts (2018)
.

Weber (2007)
introduces the notion of (elementary)
2
-topos to abstract over the typical case of a
2
-category of internal categories, suitable for a general semantics of AdapTT
2
.

6.
Perspectives

We have provided a foundational framework to understand type casting operations
in dependent type theory. This framework has close kinship to existing
classes of models, particularly comprehension
categories
Jacobs, 1993
;
Coraglia and Emmenegger, 2024b
;
Najmaei et al., 2026
, although it is closer to
established syntaxes and type systems, making it appealing for potential
implementations. Our main contribution is the systematic study of functorial
type formers in this framework, providing a general explanation of the
structural aspects of type casting. This relies on the introduction
of variable for dependent types, giving rise to an elegant
2-categorical structure which let us easily derive functoriality for
complex composite types. The work culminates in the derivation of functoriality
for a general class of indexed inductive types, from their description
in a suitable theory of signatures.

We hope our work will inform the design of novel coercion
systems in proof assistants and dependently typed
programming languages, and pave the way for further study of topics
which are both critical for practical usability and theoretically
rather poorly understood, despite longstanding interest.
Many avenues are still open for further theoretical and practical investigations.

Variations on variance

AdapTT
2
only features co- and contravariant types and type variables, leaving the field open to extensions that we sketch here.

First, allowing mixed variance in types and telescopes would support
Non-Example

4.7
.
From the analysis there, handling mixed variance, that is, types whose action on
a transformation generates some more complex data than a single adapter in one
or the other direction, seem to require working with spans.

Second, an equivariant direction characterizing types with only trivial adapters would be useful to describe types from AdapTT which cannot be directly typed in AdapTT
2
.
For instance, the identity function’s type
A\to A
needs
A
to be both co- and contravariant, which could be obtained for an equivariant
A
.
We conjecture this to be the sole obstruction to embed AdapTT into AdapTT
2
.

Third, strict positivity of inductive types can itself be expressed using variance
Escot et al., 2023
.
Doing so with the infrastructure of AdapTT
2
would simplify the description of inductive types, and enable nested types,
i.e.
inductive types where a recursive occurrence appears as
parameter of another inductive type.
Moreover,
Escot et al.
report that their
Agda
implementation suffers from
interactions of variance with structural subtyping, which would
could alleviated using AdapTT
2
.

Generating adapters

Beyond the examples of TT
obs
, MLTT
coe
and CastCIC, many more instances of ground adapters would make sense as extensions AdapTT: user-defined coercions à la
Rocq
or
Lean
, cumulativity as an adapter

\Ad(\mathcal{U}_{i},\mathcal{U}_{j})
between universes whenever
i\leq j
, record coercions as a building block for algebraic hierarchies
Pollack, 2000
, refinement between subset types as employed in PVS
Shankar and Owre, 2000
, F
\star

Swamy et al., 2016a
and liquid type systems
Rondon et al., 2008
.
AdapTT gives a good lens to (re)analyze these instances of type casts,
and the framework provided by AdapTT
2
could greatly help in obtaining enhanced, structural versions,
well-behaved thanks to definitional functoriality.

Metatheory & Implementation

We did not attempt to prove normalization or decidability of type-checking for our languages, though we conjecture these properties should hold.
Indeed, very few such proofs cover a general class of inductive types, limiting the interest of the challenge.
However, despite its rich equational theory, conversion in AdapTT
2
is intentional enough to be decidable.
At a high level, apart from the unproblematic β and η rules, deciding the additional functor laws should follow the strategy of MLTT
map

Laurent et al., 2024

and reduce to functoriality of adapters at inductive types that can be dealt
by using ideas from
\AtNextCite
Allais et al. (2013)
ν-equations.

An important insight by
McBride and Nordvall Forsberg (2021)
, which does not appear in
our algebraic approach to typing,
is the close relationship between adapters and bidirectional typing:
adapters naturally mediate
the phase change between inference and checking
We expect that these aspects would surface in a future implementation of AdapTT and AdapTT
2
.

Data Availability Statement

The
Agda
formalisation used to guide the rules’ design by
type-checking them is freely available on Zenodo
Adjedj et al., 2025
.
